**Washington, D.C.**, officially the **District of Columbia** and commonly known as **Washington**, is the [capital city](181337.md) of the [United States](3434750.md), and its only [federal district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_district_of_the_United_States).[^cite_note-10]

The city stands on the north-east bank of the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River), across from the state of [Virginia](32432.md), surrounded by [Maryland](18858.md) to its north, east, and south.[^cite_note-11] The district was named for [Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_\(personification\)), the female [personification of the American nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_personification), through which human form and attributes are applied to the United States. "Washington" is a more recent addition to the city's common name, and is from the name of [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington), the first [U.S. president](24113.md).

The [U.S. Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Constitution) in 1789 called for the creation of a federal district under [exclusive jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_home_rule) of the [U.S. Congress](31756.md), which would become a constituent part of the Union that is not part of any [state](18618239.md) or [territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_the_United_States). The [Residence Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residence_Act), adopted on July 16, 1790, approved the creation of the [capital district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_district) along the Potomac River, and is considered the city's founding date. In 1800, when the capital was moved from [Philadelphia](50585.md), the [6th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_United_States_Congress) started meeting in the then-unfinished [Capitol Building](31979.md), and the second president, [John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams), moved into the newly finished [White House](33057.md). In 1801, the District of Columbia, formerly part of Maryland and Virginia and including the existing settlements of [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) and [Alexandria](18940583.md), was officially made the federal district; initially, the city was a separate settlement within the larger district. In 1846, Congress reduced the size of the district when it [returned the land that Virginia had ceded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_retrocession), including the city of Alexandria. In 1871, it made the entire district into a [single municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Organic_Act_of_1871). There have been several failed efforts to [reduce the district further and admit the rest as a state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_statehood_movement) since the 1880s, including a [statehood bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,_D.C.,_Admission_Act) that passed the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) in 2021 but was not adopted by the [U.S. Senate](24909346.md).

[Designed in 1791](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Enfant_Plan) by [Pierre Charles L'Enfant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Charles_L'Enfant), the city is split into [quadrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrants_of_Washington,_D.C.) that meet at the Capitol Building, with 131 [neighborhoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neighborhoods_in_Washington,_D.C.) overall. As of the [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census), the city's population was 689,545.[^cite_note-QuickFacts-2] During the workweek, commuters from the city's Maryland and Virginia suburbs increase the city's daytime population to more than one million.[^cite_note-U.S._Department_of_Commerce-12] The [Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area), which includes parts of Maryland, Virginia, and [West Virginia](32905.md), is the country's [seventh-largest metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_statistical_area), with a 2023 population of 6.3 million residents.[^cite_note-MetroEst-5] [A locally elected mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_District_of_Columbia) and [13-member council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_District_of_Columbia) have governed the district since 1973, though Congress retains the power to overturn local laws. Washington, D.C., residents do not have voting representation in Congress, but elect a single [non-voting congressional delegate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-voting_members_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) to the U.S. House of Representatives. The city's voters choose three [presidential electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_electors) in accordance with the [Twenty-third Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-third_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution), passed in 1961.

Washington, D.C., anchors the southern end of the [Northeast megalopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_megalopolis). As the seat of the [U.S. federal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._federal_government), the city is an important [world political capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caput_Mundi).[^cite_note-13] The city hosts buildings that house federal government headquarters, including the [White House](33057.md), U.S. Capitol, [Supreme Court Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court_Building), and [multiple federal departments and agencies](58232.md). The city is home to many national [monuments and museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Washington,_D.C.), located most prominently on or around the National Mall, including the [Jefferson Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Memorial), [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial), and [Washington Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monument). It hosts [177 foreign embassies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_in_Washington,_D.C.) and the global headquarters of the [World Bank](36752.md), [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), [Organization of American States](22788.md), and other international organizations. Home to many of the nation's largest industry associations, non-profit organizations, and [think tanks](37101.md), the city is known as a [lobbying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobbying_in_the_United_States) hub, which is centered on and around [K Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K_Street_\(Washington,_D.C.\)).[^cite_note-14] It is also among the country's top tourist destinations; in 2022, it had an estimated 20.7 million domestic[^cite_note-15] and 1.2 million international visitors, the seventh-most among U.S. cities.[^cite_note-16]

## History

Main article: [History of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Washington,_D.C.)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Washington,_D.C.)

### Pre-founding

The [Algonquian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquian_languages)-speaking [Piscataway people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piscataway_people) inhabited present-day Washington, D.C. and lands around the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River) when Europeans first arrived and [colonized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas) the region in the early 17th century. The [Nacotchtank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacotchtank), also called the Nacostines by [Catholic missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_missionaries), maintained settlements around the [Anacostia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_River) in present-day Washington, D.C. Conflicts with European colonists and neighboring tribes ultimately displaced the Piscataway people, some of whom established a new settlement in 1699 near [Point of Rocks, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Point_of_Rocks,_Maryland).[^cite_note-17]

### Founding



In 1800, the [United States Congress](31756.md) began assembling in the new [United States Capitol](31979.md) after the nation's capital was moved from [Philadelphia](50585.md), which served as the capital during the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution) and again from 1790 to 1800.

During the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution) and [Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War), [nine cities served as capitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_capitals_in_the_United_States#National_capitals) to the [Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Congress) and under the [Articles of Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation). Following independence, New York City briefly served as the first capital under the [Constitution](31644.md) before the capital returned to [Philadelphia](50585.md), where it remained from 1790 to 1800.[^cite_note-18]

On October 6, 1783, after the [Pennsylvania Mutiny of 1783](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Mutiny_of_1783) forced the capital to move briefly from Philadelphia to present-day [Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University) in [Princeton, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton,_New_Jersey), Congress resolved to consider a new location for it.[^cite_note-JCC120-19] The following day, [Elbridge Gerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elbridge_Gerry) of Massachusetts moved "that buildings for the use of Congress be erected on the banks of the [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) near [Trenton](57843.md), or of the [Potomac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River), near [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), provided a suitable district can be procured on one of the rivers as aforesaid, for a federal town".[^cite_note-JCC127-20]

In [Federalist No. 43](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalist_No._43), published on January 23, 1788, [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison) argued that the new [federal government](195149.md) would need authority over a national capital to provide for its own maintenance and safety.[^cite_note-21] The Pennsylvania Mutiny of 1783 emphasized the need for the national government to not rely on any state for its own security.[^cite_note-Crew-22]<sup>: 66 </sup>

[Article One, Section Eight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_One_of_the_United_States_Constitution#Section_8:_Powers_of_Congress) of the Constitution permits the establishment of a "District (not exceeding ten miles square) as may, by cession of particular states, and the acceptance of Congress, become the seat of the government of the United States".[^cite_note-23] The Constitution, however, does not specify a location for the capital. In the [Compromise of 1790](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compromise_of_1790), Madison, [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton), and [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) agreed that the federal government would pay each state's remaining Revolutionary War debts in exchange for establishing the new national capital in the [Southern United States](179553.md).[^cite_note-Crew-22]<sup>: 124–127 </sup>[^cite_note-24]

On July 9, 1790, Congress passed the [Residence Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residence_Act), which approved creating a national capital on the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River). Under the Residence Act, the exact location was to be selected by President [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington), who signed the bill into law on July 16, 1790. Formed from land donated by Maryland and Virginia, the initial shape of the federal district was a square measuring 10 miles (16 km) on each side and totaling 100 square miles (259 km<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-Crew-22]<sup>: 89–92 </sup>[^cite_note-25]

Two pre-existing settlements were included in the territory, the port of [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), founded in 1751,[^cite_note-26] and the [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port) city of [Alexandria, Virginia](18940583.md), founded in 1749.[^cite_note-27] In 1791 and 1792, a team led by [Andrew Ellicott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Ellicott), including Ellicott's brothers [Joseph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Ellicott) and [Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Ellicott) and African American [astronomer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomer) [Benjamin Banneker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Banneker), whose parents had been enslaved, surveyed the borders of the federal district and placed [boundary stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boundary_markers_of_the_original_District_of_Columbia) at every mile point; many of these stones are still standing.[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] Both Maryland and Virginia were [slave states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_states), and slavery [existed in the district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_District_of_Columbia) from its founding, and much of its construction likely relied in significant part on slave labor; slave receipts have been found for the White House, Capitol Building, and establishment of [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University). The city became an important [slave market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_market) and a center of the nation's [internal slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_trade_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31]

After its survey, the new [federal city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_city) was constructed on the north bank of the Potomac River, east of Georgetown and centered on [Capitol Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitol_Hill). On September 9, 1791, three commissioners overseeing the capital's construction named the city in honor of President Washington. At the same time, the federal district was named Columbia,[^cite_note-Crew-22]<sup>: 101 </sup> which was [a poetic name for the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_\(personification\)) commonly used at the time, being the feminine form of the name "Colombus", in reference to [Christopher Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus).[^cite_note-32] Congress held its first session there on November 17, 1800.[^cite_note-33]

Congress passed the [District of Columbia Organic Act of 1801](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Organic_Act_of_1801), which officially organized the district and placed the entire territory under the [exclusive control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_home_rule) of the federal government. The area within the district was organized into two counties, the [County of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_County,_D.C.) to the east and north of the Potomac River and the [County of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_of_Alexandria) to the west and south.[^cite_note-Crew-22]<sup>: 717 </sup> After the Act's passage, citizens in the district were no longer considered residents of Maryland or Virginia, and their representation in Congress ended.[^cite_note-34]

### Burning during War of 1812

Main article: [Burning of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_of_Washington)



After their victory at the [Battle of Bladensburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bladensburg) in 1814, the British burned the [White House](33057.md) and other federal buildings during a one-day occupation of Washington.

On August 24, 1814, during the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812), [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland) forces occupied Washington after defeating an American army at the [Battle of Bladensburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bladensburg). In retaliation for acts of destruction by American troops in the [Canadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadas), the British set fire to federal buildings in the city, gutting the [Capitol](31979.md), [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress), [Treasury Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasury_Building_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), and [White House](33057.md) in what became known as the [burning of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_of_Washington). The damage of the city's burning could have been more extensive, but a storm forced the British to evacuate the city after just 24 hours.[^cite_note-35] Most federal buildings were repaired quickly, but the Capitol, which was then still under construction, was not completed in its current form until 1868.[^cite_note-36]

### Retrocession and the Civil War

Main articles: [District of Columbia retrocession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_retrocession) and [Washington, D.C., in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,_D.C.,_in_the_American_Civil_War)



The [U.S. Capitol dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Capitol_dome) was under construction during [Lincoln's first inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln's_first_inaugural_address) on March 4, 1861, five weeks before the start of the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War).

In the 1830s, the district's southern territory of [Alexandria](18940583.md) declined economically, due in part to its neglect by Congress.[^cite_note-richards-37] Alexandria was a major market in the [domestic slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_slave_trade) and pro-slavery residents feared that [abolitionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States) in Congress would end [slavery in the district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_District_of_Columbia). Alexandria's citizens petitioned Virginia to retake the land it had donated to form the district, a process known as [retrocession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_retrocession).[^cite_note-38]

The [Virginia General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_General_Assembly) voted in February 1846, to accept the return of Alexandria. On July 9, 1846, Congress went further, agreeing to return all territory that Virginia had ceded to the district during its formation. This left the district's area consisting only of the portion originally donated by Maryland.[^cite_note-richards-37] Confirming the fears of pro-slavery Alexandrians, the [Compromise of 1850](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compromise_of_1850) outlawed the slave trade in the district, although not slavery itself.[^cite_note-39]

The outbreak of the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) in 1861 led to the expansion of the federal government and notable growth in the city's population, including a large influx of freed slaves.[^cite_note-Dodd-40] President [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) signed the [Compensated Emancipation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compensated_Emancipation_Act) in 1862, which ended slavery in the district, freeing about 3,100 slaves in the district nine months before the [Emancipation Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_Proclamation).[^cite_note-emancipation-41] In 1868, Congress granted the district's [African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American) male residents the right to vote in municipal elections.[^cite_note-Dodd-40]

### Growth and redevelopment

See also: [City Beautiful movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Beautiful_movement)



The [Eisenhower Executive Office Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eisenhower_Executive_Office_Building), built between 1871 and 1888, was the world's largest office building until 1943, when it was surpassed by [The Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon).

By 1870, the district's population had grown 75% in a decade to nearly 132,000 people,[^cite_note-histpop-42] yet the city still lacked paved roads and basic sanitation. Some members of Congress suggested moving the capital farther west, but President [Ulysses S. Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant) refused to consider the proposal.[^cite_note-43]

In the [Organic Act of 1871](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_Act_of_1871), Congress repealed the individual charters of the cities of Washington and [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), abolished [Washington County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_County,_D.C.), and created a new territorial government for the whole District of Columbia.[^cite_note-44] These steps made "the city of Washington...legally indistinguishable from the District of Columbia."[^cite_note-Tikkanen-2023-45]

In 1873, President Grant appointed [Alexander Robey Shepherd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Robey_Shepherd) as Governor of the District of Columbia. Shepherd authorized large projects that modernized the city but bankrupted its government. In 1874, Congress replaced the territorial government with an appointed three-member board of commissioners.[^cite_note-46]

In 1888, the city's [first motorized streetcars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streetcars_in_Washington,_D.C.) began service. Their introduction generated growth in areas of the district beyond the City of Washington's original boundaries, leading to an expansion of the district over the next few decades.[^cite_note-neighborhoods-47] Georgetown's street grid and other administrative details were formally merged with those of the City of Washington in 1895.[^cite_note-Tindall-48] However, the city had poor housing and strained public works; this led it to become the first city in the nation to undergo [urban renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_renewal) projects as part of the [City Beautiful movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Beautiful_movement) in the early 20th century.[^cite_note-ramroth-49]

The City Beautiful movement built heavily upon the already-implemented [L'Enfant Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Enfant_Plan), with the new [McMillan Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McMillan_Plan) leading urban development in the city throughout the movement. Much of the old [Victorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_architecture) [Mall](167796.md) was replaced with modern [Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) and [Beaux-Arts architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaux-Arts_architecture); these designs are still prevalent in the city's governmental buildings today.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51]

Increased federal spending under the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal) in the 1930s led to the construction of new government buildings, memorials, and museums in the district,[^cite_note-52] though the chairman of the House Subcommittee on District Appropriations, [Ross A. Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_A._Collins) of [Mississippi](16949861.md), justified cuts to funds for welfare and education for local residents by saying that "my constituents wouldn't stand for spending money on niggers."[^cite_note-53]

[World War II](32927.md) led to an expansion of federal employees in the city;[^cite_note-54] by 1950, the district's population reached its peak of 802,178 residents.[^cite_note-histpop-42]

### Civil rights and home rule era

See also: [1968 Washington, D.C., riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Washington,_D.C.,_riots) and [District of Columbia home rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_home_rule)



The [March on Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_for_Jobs_and_Freedom) at the [Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial_Reflecting_Pool) on August 28, 1963

The [Twenty-third Amendment to the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-third_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) was ratified in 1961, granting the district three votes in the [Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_College_\(United_States\)) for the election of president and vice president, but still not affording the city's residents representation in Congress.[^cite_note-55]

After the [assassination of civil rights leader Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.) on April 4, 1968, [riots broke out in the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Washington,_D.C.,_riots), primarily in the U Street, 14th Street, 7th Street, and H Street corridors, which were predominantly black residential and commercial areas. The riots raged for three days until more than 13,600 federal troops and [Washington, D.C., Army National Guardsmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_National_Guard) stopped the violence. Many stores and other buildings were burned, and rebuilding from the riots was not completed until the late 1990s.[^cite_note-56]

In 1973, Congress enacted the [District of Columbia Home Rule Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Home_Rule_Act) providing for an elected mayor and 13-member council for the district.[^cite_note-57] In 1975, [Walter Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Washington) became the district's first elected and first black mayor.[^cite_note-58]

### Statehood movement

Main article: [District of Columbia statehood movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_statehood_movement)

Since the 1980s, the [D.C. statehood movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._statehood_movement) has grown in prominence. In 2016, a [referendum on D.C. statehood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Washington,_D.C.,_statehood_referendum) showed 85% support among Washington, D.C., voters for it to become the nation's [51st state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/51st_state). In March 2017, the city's congressional delegate [Eleanor Holmes Norton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleanor_Holmes_Norton) introduced a bill for statehood. Reintroduced in 2019 and 2021 as the [Washington, D.C., Admission Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,_D.C.,_Admission_Act), the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) passed it in April 2021.[^cite_note-59] After not progressing in the Senate, the statehood bill was introduced again in January 2023.[^cite_note-60] The bill would have made D.C. into a state with one representative and two senators, with the name Washington, Douglass Commonwealth (thus keeping the abbreviation Washington, D.C.).[^cite_note-61] The legalities, reasons, and impact of statehood have been heavily debated in the 2020s.[^cite_note-sthd101-62]

### 2025 federal takeover

Main article: [Executive Order 14333](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_14333)

See also: [2025 deployment of federal forces in the United States](80838047.md)

On August 11, 2025, [President Donald Trump](78298108.md) switched control of the [Metropolitan Police Department of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Police_Department_of_the_District_of_Columbia) from the city government of Washington, D.C., to the federal government, invoking section 740 of the [District of Columbia Home Rule Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Home_Rule_Act). Trump also deployed [federal law enforcement agencies](16252377.md) and the [District of Columbia National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_National_Guard) in response to what he said was "rampant crime" in the city.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Washington,_D.C.)



The [Washington Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monument) viewed from the [Tidal Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_Basin) during the [National Cherry Blossom Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cherry_Blossom_Festival) in April 2018



Washington, D.C., is located on the north side of the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River). It is bordered on three sides by [Maryland](18858.md) and by [Northern Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Virginia) to its southwest.

Washington, D.C., is located in the [Mid-Atlantic region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-Atlantic_region) of the [U.S. East Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._East_Coast). The city has a total area of 68.34 square miles (177 km<sup>2</sup>), of which 61.05 square miles (158.1 km<sup>2</sup>) is land and 7.29 square miles (18.9 km<sup>2</sup>) (10.67%) is water.[^cite_note-65] The district is bordered by [Montgomery County, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_County,_Maryland), to the northwest; [Prince George's County, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_George's_County,_Maryland), to the east; [Arlington County, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington_County,_Virginia), to the west; and [Alexandria, Virginia](18940583.md), to the south.

The south bank of the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River) forms the district's border with Virginia and has two major tributaries, the [Anacostia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_River) and [Rock Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Creek_\(Potomac_River_tributary\)).[^cite_note-66] [Tiber Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiber_Creek), a natural watercourse that once passed through the [National Mall](167796.md), was fully enclosed underground during the 1870s.[^cite_note-67] The creek also formed a portion of the now-filled [Washington City Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_City_Canal), which allowed passage through the city to the Anacostia River from 1815 until the 1850s.[^cite_note-68] The [Chesapeake and Ohio Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chesapeake_and_Ohio_Canal) starts in [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) and was used during the 19th century to bypass the [Little Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Falls_\(Potomac_River\)) of the Potomac River, located at the northwest edge of the city at the [Atlantic Seaboard fall line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Seaboard_fall_line).[^cite_note-69]

The highest natural elevation in the district is 409 feet (125 m) [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_sea_level) at [Fort Reno Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Reno_Park) in upper [northwest Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Washington,_D.C.)[^cite_note-70] The lowest point is sea level at the Potomac River.[^cite_note-71] The geographic center of Washington is near the intersection of 4th and L streets NW.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-legend-74]

### Parks



[Rock Creek Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Creek_Park), the city's largest park, stretches across [Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_\(Washington,_D.C.\)).



The *[Cascading Waterfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cascading_Waterfall)* at [Meridian Hill Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian_Hill_Park) in [Meridian Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian_Hill)

There are many parks, gardens, squares, and circles throughout Washington. The city has 683 parks and greenspaces, comprising 7,464 acres (30.21 km<sup>2</sup>), about 20% of its land area. Consequently, 99% of residents live within a 10-minute walk of a park. According to the nonprofit [Trust for Public Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trust_for_Public_Land), in 2023 Washington ranked first among the 100 largest U.S. cities for its public parks, based on indicators such as accessibility, the share of land reserved for parks, and the amount invested in green spaces.[^cite_note-\:0-75][^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77]

The [National Park Service](161535.md) manages most of the 9,122 acres (36.92 km<sup>2</sup>) of city land owned by the U.S. government.[^cite_note-78] [Rock Creek Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Creek_Park), located in Northwest D.C., is the largest park in the city, with 1,754 acres (7.10 km<sup>2</sup>) of urban forest extending 9.3 miles (15.0 km) through a stream valley that bisects the city. Established in 1890, it is the country's fourth-oldest national park and is home to a variety of plant and animal species, including raccoon, deer, owls, and coyotes.[^cite_note-79] Other National Park Service properties include the [Chesapeake and Ohio Canal National Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chesapeake_and_Ohio_Canal_National_Historical_Park), the [National Mall and Memorial Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Mall_and_Memorial_Parks), [Fort Dupont Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Dupont_Park), [Meridian Hill Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian_Hill_Park), [Kenilworth Park and Aquatic Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenilworth_Park_and_Aquatic_Gardens), and [Anacostia Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_Park).[^cite_note-80] The [District of Columbia Department of Parks and Recreation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Department_of_Parks_and_Recreation) maintains the city's 900 acres (3.6 km<sup>2</sup>) of athletic fields and playgrounds, 40 swimming pools, and 68 recreation centers.[^cite_note-81] The [U.S. Department of Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_Agriculture) operates the 446-acre (1.80 km<sup>2</sup>) [United States National Arboretum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_National_Arboretum), a dense [arboretum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arboretum) in Northeast D.C. filled with gardens and trails. Its most notable landmark is the [National Capitol Columns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capitol_Columns) monument.[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84]

There are several river islands in Washington, D.C., including [Theodore Roosevelt Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt_Island) in the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River), which hosts the [Theodore Roosevelt National Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt_Island#National_memorial) and a number of trails.[^cite_note-85] [Columbia Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Island_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), also in the Potomac, is home to the [Lyndon Baines Johnson Memorial Grove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_Baines_Johnson_Memorial_Grove), the [Navy – Merchant Marine Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navy_%E2%80%93_Merchant_Marine_Memorial), and a marina. [Kingman Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingman_Island), in the [Anacostia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_River), is home to [Langston Golf Course](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langston_Golf_Course) and a public park with trails.[^cite_note-86]

[West Potomac Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Potomac_Park) includes the parkland that extends south of the [Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial_Reflecting_Pool), from the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial) to the grounds of the [Washington Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monument) (see [§ National Mall and Tidal Basin](#National_Mall_and_Tidal_Basin) below).[^cite_note-87] Located on the northern side of the White House, [Lafayette Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafayette_Square_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) is a historic public square. It has been the site of many protests, marches, and speeches. The houses bordering Lafayette Square have served as the home to many notable figures.[^cite_note-88] Other parks, gardens, and squares include [Dumbarton Oaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dumbarton_Oaks), [Meridian Hill Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian_Hill_Park), [the Yards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Yards_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), [Lincoln Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Park_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), [Franklin Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_Square_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), [McPherson Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McPherson_Square), and [Farragut Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farragut_Square).[^cite_note-89] There are a large number of [traffic circles and circle parks in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_circles_in_Washington,_D.C.), including [Dupont Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dupont_Circle), [Logan Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logan_Circle_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), and [Thomas Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Circle).[^cite_note-90]

### Climate

See also: [Climate change in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Washington,_D.C.)



The [U.S. Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Capitol) during the [February 5–6, 2010 North American blizzard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_5%E2%80%936,_2010_North_American_blizzard)

Washington's climate is temperate [humid subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *Cfa*), or [oceanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) ([Trewartha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trewartha_climate_classification): *Do* bordering *Cf* downtown)[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] Winters tend to be chilly, with some snow of varying intensity, while summers are hot and humid. The district is in plant [hardiness zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardiness_zone) 8a near downtown, and zone 7b elsewhere in the city.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-WaPo_temps-94]

Summers are hot and humid with a July daily average of 79.8 °F (26.6 °C) and average daily relative humidity around 66%, which can cause moderate personal discomfort. Heat indices regularly approach 100 °F (38 °C) at the height of summer.[^cite_note-95] The combination of heat and humidity in the summer brings very frequent thunderstorms, some of which occasionally produce tornadoes in the area.[^cite_note-96]

Blizzards affect Washington once every four to six years on average. The most violent storms, known as [nor'easters](402179.md), often impact large regions of the [East Coast](89126.md).[^cite_note-dcwinter-97] From [January 27 to 28, 1922](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knickerbocker_storm), the city officially received 28 inches (71 cm) of snowfall, the largest snowstorm since official measurements began in 1885.[^cite_note-98] According to notes kept at the time, the city received between 30 and 36 inches (76 and 91 cm) from a snowstorm in January 1772.[^cite_note-DOC-99]

Hurricanes or their remnants occasionally impact the area in late summer and early fall. However, they usually are weak by the time they reach Washington, partly due to the city's inland location.[^cite_note-100] Flooding of the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River), however, caused by a combination of high tide, storm surge, and runoff, has been known to cause extensive property damage in the [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) neighborhood of the city.[^cite_note-101] Precipitation occurs throughout the year.[^cite_note-WMO_1961–90_KDCA-102]

The highest recorded temperature was 106 °F (41 °C) on August 6, 1918, and on July 20, 1930.[^cite_note-Samenow-103] More recently, Washington, D.C. recorded a high temperature of 105 °F (41 °C) on July 7, 2012.[^cite_note-104] The lowest recorded temperature was −15 °F (−26 °C) on [February 11, 1899](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Blizzard_of_1899#Arctic_cold), right before the [Great Blizzard of 1899](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Blizzard_of_1899).[^cite_note-dcwinter-97] During a typical year, the city averages about 37 days at or above 90 °F (32 °C) and 64 nights at or below the freezing mark (32 °F or 0 °C).[^cite_note-NWS_Baltimore/Washington_(LWX)-105] On average, the first day with a minimum at or below freezing is November 18 and the last day is March 27.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107]

## Cityscape

See also: [Streets and highways of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streets_and_highways_of_Washington,_D.C.); [Neighborhoods in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neighborhoods_in_Washington,_D.C.); and [List of tallest buildings in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Washington,_D.C.)



The [L'Enfant Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Enfant_Plan) for the city, developed in 1791 by [Pierre Charles L'Enfant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Charles_L'Enfant)



In 1830, the [Chesapeake and Ohio Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chesapeake_and_Ohio_Canal) was extended to [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)).



An aerial view of [Northwest Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) in June 2018

Washington, D.C., was a [planned city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_city), and many of the city's [street grids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_grid) were developed in that initial plan. In 1791, President [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) commissioned [Pierre Charles L'Enfant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Charles_L'Enfant), a French-born military engineer and artist, to design the new capital. He enlisted the help of Isaac Roberdeau, [Étienne Sulpice Hallet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tienne_Sulpice_Hallet) and Scottish surveyor [Alexander Ralston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Ralston) to help lay out the city plan.[^cite_note-c109-113] The [L'Enfant Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Enfant_Plan) featured broad streets and avenues radiating out from rectangles, providing room for open space and landscaping.[^cite_note-lenfant-114]

L'Enfant was also provided a roll of maps by [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) depicting Frankfurt, Amsterdam, Strasbourg, Paris, Orleans, Bordeaux, Lyon, Marseille, Turin, and Milan.[^cite_note-115] L'Enfant's design also envisioned a garden-lined grand avenue about 1 mile (1.6 km) long and 400 feet (120 m) wide in an area that is now the [National Mall](167796.md) inspired by the grounds at [Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Versailles) and [Tuileries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuileries) Gardens.[^cite_note-116] In March 1792, President Washington dismissed L'Enfant due to conflicts with the three commissioners appointed to supervise the capital's construction. [Andrew Ellicott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Ellicott), who worked with L'Enfant in surveying the city, was then tasked with completing its design. Though Ellicott revised the original L'Enfant plans, including changing some street patterns, L'Enfant is still credited with the city's overall design.[^cite_note-Crew-22]<sup>: 101–103 </sup>

By the early 20th century, however, L'Enfant's vision of a grand national capital was marred by slums and randomly placed buildings in the city, including a railroad station on National Mall. Congress formed a special committee charged with beautifying Washington's ceremonial core.[^cite_note-ramroth-49] What became known as the [McMillan Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McMillan_Plan) was finalized in 1901 and included landscaping the Capitol grounds and National Mall, clearing slums, and establishing a new citywide park system. The plan is thought to have largely preserved L'Enfant's intended design for the city.[^cite_note-lenfant-114]

By law, the city's skyline is low and sprawling. The federal [Height of Buildings Act of 1910](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Height_of_Buildings_Act_of_1910) limits building height based on the width of the adjacent street, with maxima of 90 feet (27 m) on residential streets and 130 feet (40 m) on commercial ones.[^cite_note-Schwartzman-117][^cite_note-118] Despite popular belief, no law has ever limited buildings to the height of the United States Capitol or the 555-foot (169 m) [Washington Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monument),[^cite_note-legend-74] which remains the district's tallest structure. City leaders have cited the height restriction as a primary reason that the district has limited affordable housing and its metro area has suburban sprawl and traffic problems.[^cite_note-Schwartzman-117]

Washington, D.C., is divided into [four quadrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrants_of_Washington,_D.C.) of unequal area: [Northwest (NW)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), [Northeast (NE)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), [Southeast (SE)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), and [Southwest (SW)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest,_Washington,_D.C.). The axes bounding the quadrants radiate from the U.S. Capitol.[^cite_note-dcstreets-119] All road names include the quadrant abbreviation to indicate their location. House numbers generally correspond with the number of blocks away from the Capitol. Most streets are set out in a grid pattern with east–west streets named with letters (e.g., C Street SW), north–south streets with numbers (e.g., 4th Street NW), and diagonal avenues, many of which are [named after states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_state-named_roadways_in_Washington,_D.C.).[^cite_note-dcstreets-119]

The City of Washington was bordered on the north by Boundary Street (renamed [Florida Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Avenue) in 1890), [Rock Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Creek_\(Potomac_River_tributary\)) to the west, and the [Anacostia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_River) to the east.[^cite_note-neighborhoods-47][^cite_note-lenfant-114] Washington, D.C.'s street grid was extended, where possible, throughout the district starting in 1888.[^cite_note-120] [Georgetown's streets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_street_renaming) were renamed in 1895.[^cite_note-Tindall-48] Some streets are particularly noteworthy, including [Pennsylvania Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Avenue), which connects the [White House](33057.md) to the Capitol; and K Street, which houses the offices of many lobbying groups.[^cite_note-kstreet-121] [Constitution Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Avenue) and [Independence Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Avenue_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), located on the north and south sides of National Mall, respectively, are home to many of Washington's iconic museums, including many [Smithsonian Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution) buildings and the [National Archives Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archives_Building). Washington hosts 177 [foreign embassies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_in_Washington,_D.C.); these maintain nearly 300 buildings and more than 1,600 residential properties, many of which are on a section of [Massachusetts Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Avenue_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) informally known as [Embassy Row](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_Row).[^cite_note-embassies-122]

### Architecture

Main articles: [Architecture of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Washington,_D.C.) and [List of tallest buildings in the Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_the_Washington_metropolitan_area)



The [Jefferson Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Memorial) and many of the city's other major monuments are built in the [Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) style.

The architecture of Washington, D.C., varies greatly and is generally popular among tourists and locals. In 2007, six of the top ten buildings in the [American Institute of Architects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Institute_of_Architects)' ranking of [America's Favorite Architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America's_Favorite_Architecture) were in the city:[^cite_note-123] the [White House](33057.md), [Washington National Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_National_Cathedral), the [Jefferson Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Memorial), the [United States Capitol](31979.md), the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial), and the [Vietnam Veterans Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_Veterans_Memorial). The [neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture), [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture), [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture), and [Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_architecture) styles are reflected among these six structures and many other prominent edifices in the city.

Many government buildings, monuments, and museums along the [National Mall](167796.md) and surrounding areas are heavily inspired by [classical](52687.md) [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Roman_architecture) and [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_architecture) architecture. The designs of the White House, the U.S. Capitol, [Supreme Court Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court_Building), [Washington Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monument), [National Gallery of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Art), Lincoln Memorial, and Jefferson Memorial are all heavily drawn from these classical architectural movements and feature large pediments, domes, columns in classical order, and heavy stone walls. Notable exceptions to the city's classical-style architecture include buildings constructed in the [French Second Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Empire_\(architecture\)) style, including the [Eisenhower Executive Office Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eisenhower_Executive_Office_Building), and the modernist [Watergate complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_complex).[^cite_note-nps-124] The [Thomas Jefferson Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson_Building), the main [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress) building, and the historic [Willard Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willard_Hotel) are built in [Beaux-Arts style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaux-Arts_style), popular throughout the world in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-Denby-126] [Meridian Hill Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian_Hill_Park) contains a [cascading waterfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cascading_Waterfall) with [Italian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Renaissance)-style architecture.[^cite_note-127]



[Contemporary architecture](3970948.md) at [CityCenterDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CityCenterDC) in [Downtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_\(Washington,_D.C.\))

[Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_architecture), [Postmodern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodern_architecture), [contemporary](3970948.md), and other non-classical architectural styles are also seen in the city. The [National Museum of African American History and Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_African_American_History_and_Culture) deeply contrasts the stone-based neoclassical buildings on the [National Mall](167796.md) with a design that combines modern engineering with heavy inspiration from [African art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_art).[^cite_note-taylorthorny-128] The interior of the [Washington Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Metro) stations and the [Hirshhorn Museum and Sculpture Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirshhorn_Museum_and_Sculpture_Garden) are designed with strong influence from the 20th-century [Brutalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brutalism) movement.[^cite_note-129] The [Smithsonian Institution Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution_Building) is built of [Seneca red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seneca_Quarry) [sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone) in the [Norman Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_architecture) style.[^cite_note-130] The [Old Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Post_Office_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) building, located on [Pennsylvania Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Avenue) and completed in 1899, was the first building in the city to have a [steel frame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_frame) structure and the first to use electrical wiring in its design.[^cite_note-131]

Notable [contemporary](3970948.md) residential buildings, restaurants, shops, and office buildings in the city include [the Wharf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wharf_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) on the [Southwest Waterfront](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Waterfront), [Navy Yard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navy_Yard_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) along the [Anacostia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_River), and [CityCenterDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CityCenterDC) in [Downtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)). The Wharf has seen the construction of several high-rise office and residential buildings overlooking the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River). Additionally, restaurants, bars, and shops have been opened at street level. Many of these buildings have a modern glass exterior and heavy curvature.[^cite_note-wapo1-132][^cite_note-wtop1-133] CityCenterDC is home to Palmer Alley, a pedestrian-only walkway, and houses several apartment buildings, restaurants, and luxury-brand storefronts with streamlined glass and metal facades.[^cite_note-DietschMarch-134]



[Victorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_architecture) houses in [Dupont Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dupont_Circle)

Outside Downtown D.C., architectural styles are more varied. Historic buildings are designed primarily in the [Queen Anne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Anne_style_architecture_in_the_United_States), [Châteauesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teauesque), [Richardsonian Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richardsonian_Romanesque), [Georgian Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture), [Beaux-Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaux-Arts_architecture), and a variety of [Victorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_architecture) styles. Rowhouses are prominent in areas developed after the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) and typically follow [Federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_architecture) and late Victorian designs.[^cite_note-135] [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\))'s [Old Stone House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Stone_House_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), built in 1765, is the oldest-standing building in the city.[^cite_note-136] Founded in 1789, [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University) features a mix of Romanesque and [Gothic Revival architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_Revival_architecture).[^cite_note-nps-124] The [Ronald Reagan Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Building) is the largest building in the district with a total area of about 3.1 million square feet (288,000 m<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-137] [Washington Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Union_Station) is designed in a combination of architectural styles. Its Great Hall has elaborate gold leaf designs along the ceilings and the hall includes several decorative classical-style statues.[^cite_note-138]

The [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Census_Bureau) estimates that the district's population was 705,749 as of July 2019, up more than 100,000 people since the [2010 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census). When measured decade-over-decade, this shows growth since 2000, following a half-century of population decline.[^cite_note-148] Washington was the [24th-most populous place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population) in the United States as of 2010.[^cite_note-149] According to data from 2010, commuters from the suburbs boost the district's daytime population past one million.[^cite_note-150] If the district were a state, it would rank [49th in population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_population), ahead of [Vermont](32578.md) and [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming).[^cite_note-quickfacts-151]



Map of racial distribution in the [Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area), according to the [2010 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_U.S._census); each dot represents 25 people: **White**, **Black**, **Asian**, **Hispanic** or **Other** (yellow)

The [Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area), which includes the district and surrounding suburbs, is the [sixth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_of_United_States_Metropolitan_Statistical_Areas) metropolitan area in the U.S., with an estimated six million residents as of 2016.[^cite_note-152] When the Washington area is included with [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore) and its suburbs, it forms the vast [Washington–Baltimore combined statistical area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington%E2%80%93Baltimore_combined_statistical_area). With a population exceeding 9.8 million residents in 2020, it is the [third-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_combined_statistical_areas) combined statistical area in the country.[^cite_note-153]

According to [Department of Housing and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development)'s [Annual Homeless Assessment Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Homeless_Assessment_Report_to_Congress) in 2022, there were an estimated 4,410 [homeless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeless) people in Washington, D.C.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155] The city passed a law in 2013 that requires shelter to be provided to everyone in need when the temperature drops below freezing.[^cite_note-156] Since D.C. does not have enough shelter units available, every winter it books hotel rooms in the suburbs with an average cost of around \$100 for a night. According to the D.C. Department of Human Services, during the winter of 2012 the city spent \$2,544,454 on putting homeless families in hotels,[^cite_note-157] and budgeted \$3.2 million on hotel beds in 2013.[^cite_note-158]

According to 2020 Census Bureau data, the population of Washington, D.C., was 41.4% Black or African American, 39.6% White (37.9% non-Hispanic White), 4.9% [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American), 0.5% American Indian or Alaska Native, 0.1% Native Hawaiian or Other Pacific Islander, and 5.4% Some Other Race. Individuals from two or more races made up 8.1% of the population. Hispanics of any race made up 11.3% of the district's population.[^cite_note-Census2020-139]



The city's African American population has declined since the [1968 riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Washington,_D.C.,_riots).

Washington, D.C. has had a relatively large [African American population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._cities_with_large_African-American_populations) since the city's foundation.[^cite_note-159] African American residents composed about 30% of the district's total population between 1800 and 1940.[^cite_note-histpop-42] The black population reached a peak of 70% by 1970 and has since declined as African Americans moved to the surrounding suburbs. Partly as a result of [gentrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentrification), there was a 31.4% increase in the non-Hispanic white population and an 11.5% decrease in the black population between 2000 and 2010.[^cite_note-demshift-160] According to a study by the National Community Reinvestment Coalition, the city has experienced more gentrification than any other U.S. city, with 40% of neighborhoods gentrified.[^cite_note-161]

As of 2010, about 17% of Washington, D.C. residents were age 18 or younger, which is lower than the U.S. average of 24%. However, at 34 years old, the district had the lowest median age compared to the 50 states as of 2010.[^cite_note-162] As of 2010, there were an estimated 81,734 [immigrants](4599312.md) living in Washington, D.C.[^cite_note-DCsocial-163] Major sources of immigration include [El Salvador](9356.md), [Ethiopia](187749.md), [Mexico](3966054.md), [Guatemala](17238567.md), and [China](5405.md), with a concentration of Salvadorans in the [Mount Pleasant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Pleasant,_Washington,_D.C.) neighborhood.[^cite_note-164]

As of 2010, there were 4,822 same-sex couples in the city, about 2% of total households, according to [Williams Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Williams_Institute).[^cite_note-165] Legislation [authorizing same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_District_of_Columbia) passed in 2009, and the district began issuing marriage licenses to same-sex couples in March 2010.[^cite_note-166]

Notable religious buildings in Washington, D.C.









Left to right from the top: The [Basilica of the National Shrine of the Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_the_National_Shrine_of_the_Immaculate_Conception); [Islamic Center of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Center_of_Washington); [Sixth & I Historic Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_&_I_Historic_Synagogue); and [St. John's Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._John's_Episcopal_Church,_Lafayette_Square)

As of 2007, about one-third of Washington, D.C., residents were [functionally illiterate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functionally_illiterate), greater than the national rate of about one-fifth. The city's relatively high illiteracy rate is attributed partly to immigrants who are not proficient in [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language).[^cite_note-167] As of 2011, 85% of D.C. residents age 5 and older spoke English at home as a primary language.[^cite_note-168] Half of residents had at least a four-year college degree in 2006.[^cite_note-DCsocial-163] In 2017, the median household income in D.C. was \$77,649;[^cite_note-DC_AF-169] also in 2017, D.C. residents had a [personal income per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_of_the_United_States_of_America_by_income) of \$50,832 (higher than any of the 50 states).[^cite_note-DC_AF-169][^cite_note-170] However, 19% of residents were below the poverty level in 2005, higher than any state except [Mississippi](16949861.md). In 2019, the poverty rate stood at 14.7%.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174]

As of 2010, more than 90% of Washington, D.C., residents had health insurance coverage, the second-highest rate in the nation. This is due in part to city programs that help provide insurance to low-income individuals who do not qualify for other types of coverage.[^cite_note-175] A 2009 report found that at least three percent of Washington, D.C., residents have [HIV](14170.md) or [AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS), which the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md) (CDC) characterizes as a "generalized and severe" epidemic.[^cite_note-176]

As of 2020, according to the [Association of Statisticians of American Religious Bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Statisticians_of_American_Religious_Bodies), 56% of the city's residents were adherents[^cite_note-178] of a religious body. The largest tradition represented was [Evangelical Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Protestantism) (15% of total population), followed by [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism) (12%), [Black Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Protestantism) (10%), [Mainline Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainline_Protestantism) (10%), [Judaism](15624.md) (3%), [Orthodox Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy) (2%), [Buddhism](3267529.md) (1%), and [Islam](6037917.md) (1%), with several other groups numbering less than 1%. Mainline Protestants were the largest group in 2010, Catholics in 2000, and Black Protestants in 1990.[^cite_note-ARDA-177] The city is populated with many religious buildings, including [Washington National Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_National_Cathedral), [Basilica of the National Shrine of the Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_the_National_Shrine_of_the_Immaculate_Conception), which comprises the [largest Catholic church building in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_church_buildings), the [Islamic Center of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Center_of_Washington), which was the largest [mosque](19894.md) in the Western Hemisphere when it opened in 1957, and [DSK Mariam Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DSK_Mariam_Church), one of the oldest Ethiopian Orthodox congregations in the United States. [St. John's Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._John's_Episcopal_Church,_Lafayette_Square), located off [Lafayette Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafayette_Square_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), has held services for every [U.S. president](24113.md) since [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison). The [Sixth & I Historic Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_&_I_Historic_Synagogue), built in 1908, is a [synagogue](48559.md) located in the [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Washington,_D.C.) section of the city. The [Washington D.C. Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_D.C._Temple) is a [temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_\(LDS_Church\)) of [the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints), located just outside the city in [Kensington, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kensington,_Maryland). Viewable from the [Capital Beltway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Beltway), the temple is the tallest temple of the church in existence, and is the third-largest by square footage.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of the Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Washington_metropolitan_area)



The [Eccles Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eccles_Building) on [Constitution Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Avenue), home of the [Federal Reserve](10819.md), the [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank) of the United States

In 2025, Washington, D.C's gross domestic product was \$192.6 billion and its per capita personal income was \$116,121.[^cite_note-181] As of 2011, the [Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area), including the District of Columbia as well as parts of [Virginia](32432.md), [Maryland](18858.md), and [West Virginia](32905.md), was the nation's eighth-largest metropolitan economy. Its growing and diversified economy has an increasing percentage of professional and business service jobs in addition to more traditional jobs rooted in tourism, entertainment, and government.[^cite_note-182]

Between 2009 and 2016, [gross domestic product](12594.md) per capita in Washington, D.C., consistently ranked at the very top among U.S. states.[^cite_note-capita2009-183] In 2016, at \$160,472, its GDP per capita was almost three times greater than that of [Massachusetts](1645518.md), which was ranked second in the nation (see [List of U.S. states and territories by GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_GDP)).[^cite_note-capita2009-183] As of 2022, the metropolitan statistical area's unemployment rate was 3.1%, ranking 171 out of the 389 metropolitan areas as defined by the U.S. [Bureau of Economic Analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Economic_Analysis).[^cite_note-184] The District of Columbia itself had an unemployment rate of 4.6% during the same time period.[^cite_note-185] In 2019, Washington, D.C., had the highest median household income in the U.S. at \$92,266.[^cite_note-186]

According to the district's [comprehensive annual financial reports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_annual_financial_report), the top employers by number of employees in 2022 included [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University), [Children's National Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_National_Medical_Center), [Washington Hospital Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Hospital_Center), [George Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_University), [American University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_University), [Georgetown University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University_Hospital), [Booz Allen & Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Booz_Allen_&_Hamilton), [Insperity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insperity) PEO Services, [Universal Protection Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Protection_Service), [Howard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_University), [Medstar Medical Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MedStar_Health), [George Washington University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_University_Hospital), [Catholic University of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_University_of_America), and [Sibley Memorial Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sibley_Memorial_Hospital).[^cite_note-187]

### Federal government

Main article: [Federal government of the United States](195149.md)



[Federal Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Triangle), a historic hub of [executive departments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_departments) of the [U.S. federal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._federal_government)

As of July 2022, 25% of people employed in Washington, D.C., were employed by the federal government.[^cite_note-188] Many of the region's residents are employed by companies and organizations that do work for the federal government, seek to influence federal policy, or are otherwise related to its work, including [law firms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_firm), [defense contractors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_contractor), [civilian contractors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_contractor), [nonprofit organizations](72487.md), [lobbying firms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobbying_in_the_United_States), [trade unions](17626.md), [industry trade groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_trade_group), and [professional associations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_association), many of which have their headquarters in or near the city for proximity to the federal government.

### Research and non-profit organizations

See also: [Category\:Non-profit organizations based in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Non-profit_organizations_based_in_Washington,_D.C.)



The [American Enterprise Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Enterprise_Institute), one of the city's many [think tanks](37101.md)

Washington, D.C., is a leading center for national and international research organizations, especially [think tanks](37101.md) engaged in public policy.[^cite_note-189] As of 2020, 8% of the country's think tanks are based in the city, including many of the largest and most widely cited,[^cite_note-190] including the [Carnegie Endowment for International Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Endowment_for_International_Peace), [Center for Strategic and International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Strategic_and_International_Studies), [Peterson Institute for International Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peterson_Institute_for_International_Economics), [The Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation), and [Urban Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Institute).[^cite_note-191]

Washington, D.C., is home to many non-profit organizations that engage with issues of domestic and global importance by conducting advanced research, running programs, or public advocacy. Among these organizations are the [UN Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Foundation), [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign), [Amnesty International](18947898.md), and the [National Endowment for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Endowment_for_Democracy).[^cite_note-192] Major medical research institutions include the [MedStar Washington Hospital Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MedStar_Washington_Hospital_Center) and the [Children's National Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_National_Medical_Center).[^cite_note-193]

The city is the country's primary location for international development firms, many of which contract with the D.C.-based [United States Agency for International Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Agency_for_International_Development) (USAID), the U.S. federal government's aid agency. The [American Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Red_Cross), a humanitarian agency focused on emergency relief, is also based in the city.[^cite_note-194]

### Private sector

See also: [Category\:Companies based in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Companies_based_in_Washington,_D.C.) and [Washington metropolitan area § Primary industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area#Primary_industries)

According to statistics compiled in 2011, four of the [largest 500 companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_500) in the country were based in Washington, D.C.[^cite_note-195] In the 2023 [Global Financial Centres Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Financial_Centres_Index), Washington was ranked as having the 8th most competitive financial center in the world, and fourth most competitive in the United States (after [New York City](3848717.md), [San Francisco](49728.md#Economy), and [Los Angeles](18110.md#Economy)).[^cite_note-196] Among the largest companies based in Washington, D.C., are [Fannie Mae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fannie_Mae), [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak), [Danaher Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danaher_Corporation), [FTI Consulting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FTI_Consulting), and [Hogan Lovells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hogan_Lovells).[^cite_note-197]

### Tourism



The [World War II Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_Memorial), one of many popular tourist sites located on the [National Mall](167796.md)

Tourism is the city's second-largest industry, after the federal government. In 2012, some 18.9 million visitors contributed an estimated \$4.8 billion to the local economy.[^cite_note-tourism2012-198] In 2019, the city saw 24.6 million tourists, including 1.8 million from foreign countries, who collectively spent \$8.15 billion during their stay.[^cite_note-tourism2019-199] Tourism helps many of the region's other industries, such as lodging, food and beverage, entertainment, shopping, and transportation.[^cite_note-tourism2019-199]

The city and the larger [Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area) have an array of attractions for tourists, including monuments, memorials, museums, sports events, and trails. Within the city, the [National Mall](167796.md) serves as the center of the tourism industry. It is there that many of the city's museums and monuments are located. Adjacent to the mall sits the [Tidal Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_Basin), where several major national memorials and monuments are located, including the popular [Jefferson Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Memorial). [Washington Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Union_Station) is a popular tourist spot with its multitude of restaurants and shops.

### Arts

Main article: [Theater in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater_in_Washington,_D.C.)



A performance of *[Moulin Rouge!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moulin_Rouge!_\(musical\))* at the [Kennedy Center for the Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Center_for_the_Performing_Arts)

Washington, D.C., is a national center for the arts, home to several concert halls and theaters. The [John F. Kennedy Center for the Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_Center_for_the_Performing_Arts) is home to [National Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Symphony_Orchestra), [Washington National Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_National_Opera), and the [Washington Ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Ballet). The [Kennedy Center Honors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Center_Honors) are awarded each year to those in the performing arts who have contributed greatly to the cultural life of the United States. This ceremony is often attended by the sitting [U.S. president](24113.md) and other dignitaries and celebrities.[^cite_note-202] The Kennedy Center also awards the annual [Mark Twain Prize for American Humor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Twain_Prize_for_American_Humor).[^cite_note-203]

The historic [Ford's Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford's_Theatre), the site of the [assassination of President Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_President_Abraham_Lincoln) on April 14, 1865, continues to function as a theatre and as a museum.[^cite_note-204]

The [Marine Barracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Barracks,_Washington,_D.C.) near [Capitol Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitol_Hill) houses the [United States Marine Band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Marine_Band); founded in 1798, it is the country's oldest professional musical organization.[^cite_note-205] [American march composer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_march_music) and Washington-native [John Philip Sousa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Philip_Sousa) led the Marine Band from 1880 until 1892.[^cite_note-206] Founded in 1925, the [United States Navy Band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy_Band) has its headquarters at the [Washington Navy Yard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Navy_Yard) and performs at official events and public concerts around the city.[^cite_note-207]
