Founded in 1950, [Arena Stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arena_Stage) achieved national attention and spurred growth in the city's independent theater movement, which now includes the [Shakespeare Theatre Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shakespeare_Theatre_Company), [Woolly Mammoth Theatre Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolly_Mammoth_Theatre_Company), and [Studio Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studio_Theatre_\(Washington,_D.C.\)).[^cite_note-208] Arena Stage reopened after a renovation and expansion in the city's emerging [Southwest waterfront area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Waterfront,_Washington,_D.C.) in 2010.[^cite_note-209] The [GALA Hispanic Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GALA_Hispanic_Theatre), now housed in the historic [Tivoli Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tivoli_Theatre_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) in [Columbia Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Heights_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), was founded in 1976 and is a National Center for the Latino Performing Arts.[^cite_note-210]

Other performing arts spaces in the city include the [Andrew W. Mellon Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_W._Mellon_Auditorium) in [Federal Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Triangle), the [Atlas Performing Arts Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlas_Performing_Arts_Center) on [H Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H_Street_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), the [Carter Barron Amphitheater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_Barron_Amphitheater) in [Rock Creek Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Creek_Park), [Constitution Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Hall) in [Downtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown,_Washington,_D.C.), the [Keegan Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keegan_Theatre) in [Dupont Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dupont_Circle), the [Lisner Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisner_Auditorium) in [Foggy Bottom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foggy_Bottom), the [Sylvan Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Sylvan_Theater) on the [National Mall](167796.md), and the [Warner Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warner_Theatre_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) in [Penn Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_Quarter). [National Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Theatre_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) in Downtown, which opened in 1835, is the second-longest continuously operating theater in the nation after [Walnut Street Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walnut_Street_Theatre) in [Philadelphia](50585.md), which opened in 1808.[^cite_note-211]

[U Street Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Street_Corridor) in Northwest is home to [Howard Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Theatre) and [Lincoln Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Theatre_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), which hosted music legends such as Washington, D.C. natives [Duke Ellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Ellington), [John Coltrane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Coltrane), and [Miles Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miles_Davis).[^cite_note-ustreet-212] Just east of U Street is [Shaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaw_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), which also served as a major cultural center during the [Jazz Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_Age). Intersecting with U Street is [Fourteenth Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourteenth_Street_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), which was an extension of the U Street cultural corridor during the 1920s through the 1960s. The collection of Fourteenth Street, U Street, and Shaw was the location of the [Black Renaissance in D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Renaissance_in_D.C.), which was part of the larger [Harlem Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_Renaissance). The area starting at Fourteenth Street downtown going north through U Street and east to Shaw boasts a high concentration of bars, restaurants, and theaters, and is among the city's most notable cultural and artistic areas.

The [Washington D.C. Area Film Critics Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_D.C._Area_Film_Critics_Association) (WAFCA), a group of more than 65 film critics, holds an annual awards ceremony.[^cite_note-aboutwafca-213]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Washington,_D.C.)



[Chuck Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Brown) performing [go-go](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Go-go) music

[Columbia Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Records), a major music record label in the U.S., was founded in Washington, D.C. in 1889.[^cite_note-214]<sup>: 105 </sup>

The city grew into being one of America's most important music cities in the early [Jazz Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_Age). [Duke Ellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Ellington), among the most prominent jazz composers and musicians of his time, was born and raised in Washington, and began his music career in the city. The center of the city's jazz scene during those years was [U street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Street_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) and [Shaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaw_\(Washington,_D.C.\)). Among the city's major jazz locations were the [Lincoln Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Theatre_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) and the [Howard Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Theatre).[^cite_note-215]

Washington has its own native music genre called [go-go](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Go-go); a post-funk, percussion-driven flavor of rhythm and blues that was popularized in the late 1970s by D.C. band leader [Chuck Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Brown).[^cite_note-216]

The district is an important center for [indie culture and music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indie_music_scene) in the United States. The DC-based label [Dischord Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dischord_Records), formed by [Ian MacKaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_MacKaye), frontman of [Fugazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fugazi), was one of the most crucial independent labels in the genesis of 1980s punk and eventually indie rock in the 1990s.[^cite_note-217] Modern [alternative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_rock) and indie music venues like [The Black Cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Cat_\(Washington,_D.C._nightclub\)) and the [9\:30 Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9\:30_Club) bring popular acts to the U Street area.[^cite_note-218] The hardcore punk scene in the city, known as [D.C. hardcore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._hardcore), is an important genre of D.C.'s contemporary music scene. Starting in the 1970s and flourishing in the [Adams Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams_Morgan) neighborhood, it is considered to be one of the most influential punk music movements in the country.[^cite_note-SG_13DCHC-219]

### Cuisine

Washington, D.C., is rich in fine and casual dining; some consider it among the country's best cities for dining.[^cite_note-220] The city has a diverse range of [restaurants](26437.md), including a wide variety of international cuisines. The city's [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), for example, has more than a dozen Chinese-style restaurants. The city also has many Middle Eastern, European, African, Asian, and Latin American cuisine options. D.C. is known as one of the best cities in the world for [Ethiopian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_cuisine), due largely to [Ethiopian immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopians_in_Washington,_D.C.) who arrived in the 20th century.[^cite_note-221] A part of the [Shaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaw_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) neighborhood in central D.C. is known as "Little Ethiopia" and has a high concentration of Ethiopian restaurants and shops.[^cite_note-222] The diversity of cuisine is also reflected in the city's many [food trucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_truck), which are particularly heavily concentrated along the [National Mall](167796.md), which has few other dining options.

Among the most notable Washington, D.C.-born foods is the [half-smoke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-smoke), a half-beef, half-pork sausage placed in a [hotdog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotdog)-style bun and topped with onion, chili, and cheese.[^cite_note-223] The city is also the birthplace of [mumbo sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mumbo_sauce), a [condiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condiment) similar to [barbecue sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbecue_sauce) but sweeter in flavor, often used on meat and [french fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_fries).[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] Washington, D.C., is known for popularizing the [jumbo slice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jumbo_slice) pizza, a large [New York–style pizza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York%E2%80%93style_pizza)[^cite_note-Barrett-226][^cite_note-Stern-227][^cite_note-228] with roots in the [Adams Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams_Morgan) neighborhood.[^cite_note-229]



[Ben's Chili Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben's_Chili_Bowl) on [U Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Street_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), known for its [half-smoke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-smoke), a historic staple of the city's cuisine

Among the city's signature restaurants is [Ben's Chili Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben's_Chili_Bowl), located on [U Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Street_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) since its founding in 1958. The restaurant rose to prominence as a peaceful escape during the violent [1968 race riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Washington,_D.C.,_riots) in the city. Famous for its [chili dogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_dog) and half-smokes, it has been visited by numerous presidents and celebrities over the years.[^cite_note-230] The [Georgetown Cupcake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_Cupcake) bakery became famous through its appearance on the reality T.V. show *[DC Cupcakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DC_Cupcakes)*. Another culinary hotspot is [Union Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Market) in [Northeast D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_D.C.), a former farmer's market and wholesale that now houses a large, gourmet [food hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_hall).[^cite_note-Lerner-231]

As of 2024, 25 restaurants[^cite_note-232] have received stars in [the D.C. Michelin Guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_Guide_Washington,_D.C.).[^cite_note-233] This represents the most starred restaurants *per capita* for any U.S. city, and the third-most in the world.[^cite_note-234] Several celebrity chefs have opened restaurants in the city, including [José Andrés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Andr%C3%A9s),[^cite_note-235] [Kwame Onwuachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwame_Onwuachi),[^cite_note-236] [Gordon Ramsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_Ramsay),[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238] and previously [Michel Richard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Richard).[^cite_note-239]

### Museums

See also: [List of museums in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Washington,_D.C.)



The [National Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Natural_History), the [third-most visited museum in the U.S. in 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-visited_museums_in_the_United_States), with 4.4 million visitors



The [National Gallery of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Art), the [fourth-most visited art museum in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-visited_museums_in_the_United_States) in 2023 with nearly four million visitors

Washington, D.C., is home to several of the [country's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-visited_museums_in_the_United_States) and [world's most visited museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-visited_museums). In 2022, the [National Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Natural_History) and the [National Gallery of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Art) were the two most visited museums in the country. Overall, Washington had eight of the 28 most visited museums in the U.S. in 2022. The same year, the National Museum of Natural History was the fifth-most-visited museum in the world and the National Gallery of Art was the eleventh.[^cite_note-teacom-240]

#### Smithsonian museums

See also: [List of Smithsonian museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Smithsonian_museums)

The [Smithsonian Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution), an educational foundation chartered by [Congress](31756.md) in 1846 and the world's largest research and museum complex, is responsible for maintaining most of the city's official museums and galleries.[^cite_note-241] The U.S. government partially funds the Smithsonian, and its collections are open to the public free of charge.[^cite_note-242] The Smithsonian's locations had a combined total of 30 million visits in 2013. The most visited museum is the [National Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Natural_History) on National Mall.[^cite_note-243] Other Smithsonian Institution museums and galleries on the Mall include the [National Air and Space Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Air_and_Space_Museum); the [National Museum of African Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_African_Art); the [National Museum of American History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_American_History); the [National Museum of the American Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_the_American_Indian); the [Sackler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_M._Sackler_Gallery) and [Freer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freer_Gallery_of_Art) galleries, which focus on Asian art and culture; the [Hirshhorn Museum and Sculpture Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirshhorn_Museum_and_Sculpture_Garden); the [Arts and Industries Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_and_Industries_Building); the [S. Dillon Ripley Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S._Dillon_Ripley_Center); and the [Smithsonian Institution Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution_Building), which serves as the institution's headquarters.[^cite_note-SI-244]

The [Smithsonian American Art Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smithsonian_American_Art_Museum) and the [National Portrait Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Portrait_Gallery_\(United_States\)) are housed in the [Old Patent Office Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Patent_Office_Building) near Washington's [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Washington,_D.C.).[^cite_note-245] [Renwick Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renwick_Gallery) is part of the Smithsonian American Art Museum and is located in a separate building near the [White House](33057.md). Other Smithsonian museums and galleries include [Anacostia Community Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_Community_Museum) in Southeast Washington, the [National Postal Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Postal_Museum) near [Washington Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Union_Station), and the [National Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Zoological_Park_\(United_States\)) in [Woodley Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodley_Park).[^cite_note-SI-244]

#### Other museums



The [National Building Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Building_Museum)

The [National Gallery of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Art) is on the National Mall near the Capitol and features American and European artworks. The U.S. government owns the gallery and its collections. However, they are not a part of the Smithsonian Institution.[^cite_note-246] The [National Building Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Building_Museum), which occupies the former Pension Building near [Judiciary Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_Square), was chartered by Congress and hosts exhibits on architecture, urban planning, and design.[^cite_note-247] The [Botanic Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Botanic_Garden) is a [botanical garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botanical_garden) and museum operated by the U.S. Congress that is open to the public.[^cite_note-248]

There are several private art museums in Washington, D.C., that house major collections and exhibits open to the public, such as the [National Museum of Women in the Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Women_in_the_Arts) and [The Phillips Collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Phillips_Collection) in [Dupont Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dupont_Circle), the first museum of modern art in the United States.[^cite_note-249] Other private museums in Washington include the [O Street Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Street_Museum), the [International Spy Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Spy_Museum), the [National Geographic Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Geographic_Society) Museum, and the [Museum of the Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_the_Bible). The [United States Holocaust Memorial Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Holocaust_Memorial_Museum) near the National Mall maintains exhibits, documentation, and artifacts related to the [Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust).[^cite_note-250]

### Landmarks

See also: [List of National Historic Landmarks in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_National_Historic_Landmarks_in_Washington,_D.C.) and [National Register of Historic Places listings in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places_listings_in_Washington,_D.C.)

#### National Mall and Tidal Basin



[National Mall](167796.md), a landscaped park extending from the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial) to the [United States Capitol](31979.md)



The [Vietnam Veterans Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_Veterans_Memorial), a two-acre (8,100 m

2

) site featuring two [black granite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_granite) walls engraved with the names of those service members who died or remain missing in the [Vietnam War](32611.md) designed by [Maya Lin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Lin), was initially controversial for its lack of heroic iconography, a departure from earlier memorial designs.

The [National Mall](167796.md) is a park near [Downtown Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Washington) that stretches nearly two miles from the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial) to the [United States Capitol](31979.md). The mall often hosts [political protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_protest_marches_on_Washington,_D.C.), concerts, festivals, and [presidential inaugurations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_inauguration). The Capitol grounds host the *[National Memorial Day Concert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Memorial_Day_Concert)*, held each [Memorial Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Day), and *[A Capitol Fourth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Capitol_Fourth)*, a concert held each [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(United_States\)). Both concerts are broadcast across the country on [PBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBS). In the evening on the Fourth of July, the park hosts a large [fireworks](59493.md) show.[^cite_note-251]

The [Washington Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monument) and the [Jefferson Pier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Pier) are near the center of the mall, south of the [White House](33057.md). Directly northwest of the Washington Monument is [Constitution Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Gardens), which includes a garden, park, pond, and a [memorial to the 56 signers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_to_the_56_Signers_of_the_Declaration_of_Independence) of the [United States Declaration of Independence](31874.md).[^cite_note-252] Just north of Constitution Gardens is the [Lockkeeper's House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockkeeper's_House,_C_&_O_Canal_Extension), which is the second-oldest building on the mall after the White House. The house is operated by the [National Park Service](161535.md) (NPS) and is open to the public. Also on the mall is the [National World War II Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_World_War_II_Memorial) at the east end of the [Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial_Reflecting_Pool); the [Korean War Veterans Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_War_Veterans_Memorial); and the [Vietnam Veterans Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_Veterans_Memorial).[^cite_note-nama-253]

South of the mall is the [Tidal Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_Basin), a human-made reservoir surrounded by pedestrian paths lined by Japanese cherry trees. Every spring, millions of cherry blossoms bloom, attracting visitors from across the world as part of the annual [National Cherry Blossom Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cherry_Blossom_Festival).[^cite_note-254] The [Franklin Delano Roosevelt Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_Delano_Roosevelt_Memorial), [George Mason Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Mason_Memorial), [Jefferson Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Memorial), [Martin Luther King Jr. Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Memorial), and the [District of Columbia War Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_War_Memorial) are around the Tidal Basin.[^cite_note-nama-253]

#### Other landmarks



Protesters in front of the [U.S. Supreme Court Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court_Building) in June 2022

Numerous historic landmarks are located outside the [National Mall](167796.md). Among these are the [Old Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Post_Office_\(Washington,_D.C.\)),[^cite_note-255] the [Treasury Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasury_Building_\(Washington,_D.C.\)),[^cite_note-256] [Old Patent Office Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Patent_Office_Building),[^cite_note-257] the [Washington National Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_National_Cathedral),[^cite_note-258] the [Basilica of the National Shrine of the Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_the_National_Shrine_of_the_Immaculate_Conception),[^cite_note-259] the [National World War I Memorial](5061517.md),[^cite_note-260] the [Frederick Douglass National Historic Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Douglass_National_Historic_Site),[^cite_note-261] [Lincoln's Cottage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln's_Cottage),[^cite_note-262] the [Dwight D. Eisenhower Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower_Memorial), and the [United States Navy Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy_Memorial).[^cite_note-263] [The Octagon House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Octagon_House), which was the building that President [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison) and his administration moved into following the burning of the White House during the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812), is now a historic museum and popular tourist destination.[^cite_note-264]

The [National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archives_and_Records_Administration) is headquartered in [a building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archives_Building) just north of the National Mall and houses thousands of documents important to American history, including the [Declaration of Independence](31874.md), the [Constitution](31644.md), and the [Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights).[^cite_note-265] Located in three buildings on Capitol Hill, the [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress) is the largest library complex in the world with a collection of more than 147 million books, manuscripts, and other materials.[^cite_note-266] The [United States Supreme Court](31737.md) is located immediately north of the Library of Congress. The [United States Supreme Court Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court_Building) was completed in 1935; before then, the court held sessions in the [Old Senate Chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Senate_Chamber) of the Capitol.[^cite_note-267]

[Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), located just north of the National Mall, houses [Capital One Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_One_Arena), which serves as the home arena to the [Washington Capitals](73125.md) of the [National Hockey League](21809.md) and the [Washington Wizards](72858.md) of the [National Basketball Association](22093.md), and serves as the city's primary indoor entertainment arena. Chinatown includes several Chinese restaurants and shops. The [Friendship Archway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friendship_Archway) is one of the largest Chinese ceremonial archways outside of [China](5405.md) and bears the Chinese characters for "Chinatown" below its roof.[^cite_note-268]

The [Southwest Waterfront](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Waterfront) along the Potomac River has been redeveloped in recent years and now serves as a popular cultural center. [The Wharf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wharf_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), as it is called, contains the city's historic [Maine Avenue Fish Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Avenue_Fish_Market). This is the oldest fish market currently in operation in the entire United States.[^cite_note-269] The Wharf also has many hotels, residential buildings, restaurants, shops, parks, piers, docks and marinas, and live music venues.[^cite_note-wapo1-132][^cite_note-wtop1-133]

## Sports

Main article: [Sports in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Washington,_D.C.)



[Capital One Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_One_Arena) in the city's [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Washington,_D.C.) section hosts the [Washington Capitals](73125.md), an [NHL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHL) team (pictured), and the [Washington Wizards](72858.md), an [NBA](22093.md) team



With over 30,000 participants as of 2024, the annual [Marine Corps Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Corps_Marathon), held annually in October in Washington, D.C. and [Arlington County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington_County), is the largest non-prize money marathon in the country.

[^cite_note-270]

Washington, D.C., has [four major professional men's sports teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._cities_with_teams_from_four_major_sports) and two major professional women's teams. The [Washington Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Nationals) of [Major League Baseball](38776.md) are the most popular sports team in the district, as of 2019.[^cite_note-271] They play at [Nationals Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationals_Park), which opened in 2008. The [Washington Commanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Commanders) (previously Redskins) of the [National Football League](21211.md) play at [Northwest Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Stadium) in nearby [Landover, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landover,_Maryland). The [Washington Wizards](72858.md) (previously Bullets) of the [National Basketball Association](22093.md) and the [Washington Capitals](73125.md) of the [National Hockey League](21809.md) play at [Capital One Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_One_Arena). The [Washington Mystics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Mystics) of the [Women's National Basketball Association](57721.md) play at [CareFirst Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CareFirst_Arena). [D.C. United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._United) of [Major League Soccer](71802.md) and the [Washington Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Spirit) of the [National Women's Soccer League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Soccer_League) play at [Audi Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audi_Field).

The city's teams have won a combined 14 professional league championships over their respective histories. The Washington Commanders have won two NFL Championships and three [Super Bowls](27718.md);[^cite_note-272] D.C. United has won four;[^cite_note-273] and the Washington Wizards, Washington Capitals, Washington Mystics, Washington Nationals, and Washington Spirit have each won a single championship.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275]

Other professional and semi-professional teams in Washington, D.C. include the [DC Defenders](59625699.md) of the [XFL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XFL_\(2020\)), [Old Glory DC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Glory_DC) of [Major League Rugby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Rugby), the [Washington Kastles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Kastles) of World TeamTennis, and the [D.C. Divas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._Divas) of the Independent Women's Football League. The [William H.G. FitzGerald Tennis Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_H.G._FitzGerald_Tennis_Center) in [Rock Creek Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Creek_Park) hosts the [Washington Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Open_\(tennis\)), a joint men's [ATP Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATP_Tour) [500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATP_500)- and women's [WTA Tour](1398173.md) [500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTA_500)-level tennis tournament, every summer in late July and early August. Washington, D.C. has two major annual marathon races, the [Marine Corps Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Corps_Marathon), held every autumn, and the [Rock 'n' Roll USA Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_'n'_Roll_USA_Marathon), held each spring. The Marine Corps Marathon began in 1976 and is sometimes called "The People's Marathon" because it is the largest marathon that does not offer prize money to participants.[^cite_note-276]

The district's four [NCAA Division I](669931.md) teams are the [American Eagles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Eagles) of [American University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_University), [George Washington Revolutionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_Revolutionaries) of [George Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_University), the [Georgetown Hoyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_Hoyas) of [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University), and the [Howard Bison and Lady Bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Bison_and_Lady_Bison) of [Howard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_University). The [Georgetown men's basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_Hoyas_men's_basketball) is the most notable and also plays at Capital One Arena. Washington, D.C. area's regional sports television network is [Monumental Sports Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumental_Sports_Network).[^cite_note-277]

## City government

Main article: [Government of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_District_of_Columbia)

### Politics

Main article: [Elections in the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_the_District_of_Columbia)

See also: [District of Columbia home rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_home_rule) and [List of District of Columbia symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_District_of_Columbia_symbols)



The [John A. Wilson Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_A._Wilson_Building) on [Pennsylvania Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Avenue), headquarters for much of the [Government of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_District_of_Columbia), including the offices of the [mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_District_of_Columbia) and [D.C. Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._Council)

[Article One, Section Eight of the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_One_of_the_United_States_Constitution) grants the [United States Congress](31756.md) exclusive jurisdiction over the city. The district did not have an elected local government until passage of the [1973 Home Rule Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Home_Rule_Act), which devolved certain Congressional powers to an [elected mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_District_of_Columbia) and a 13-member [Council of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_District_of_Columbia). The district has a type of [unitary authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_authority) that combines the powers of a state-level entity, a [county equivalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_equivalent), and a city. However, Congress retains the right to review and overturn laws created by the council and intervene in local affairs.[^cite_note-278] Washington, D.C., is [overwhelmingly Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_the_District_of_Columbia), [having voted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_elections_in_the_District_of_Columbia) for [Democratic](5043544.md) presidential candidates consistently since it was granted electoral votes in the [1964 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-279]

Each of the city's eight [wards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ward_\(electoral_subdivision\)) elects a single member of the council and residents elect four at-large members to represent the district as a whole. The council chair is also elected at-large.[^cite_note-280] There are 37 [Advisory Neighborhood Commissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advisory_Neighborhood_Commission) (ANCs) elected by small neighborhood districts. ANCs can issue recommendations on all issues that affect residents; government agencies take their advice under careful consideration.[^cite_note-281] The [attorney general of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_the_District_of_Columbia) is elected to a four-year term.[^cite_note-282]

Washington, D.C., observes all [federal holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_holidays_in_the_United_States) and also celebrates [Emancipation Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_Day) on April 16, which commemorates the end of slavery in the district.[^cite_note-emancipation-41] The [flag of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Washington,_D.C.), was adopted in 1938 and is a variation on George Washington's family [coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms).[^cite_note-283]

Washington, D.C., has been a member state of the [Unrepresented Nations and Peoples Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unrepresented_Nations_and_Peoples_Organization) (UNPO) since 2015.[^cite_note-284]

The idiom "[Inside the Beltway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inside_the_Beltway)" is a reference used to describe discussions of national political issues inside of Washington, D.C., by way of geographically demarcating the region inside the [Capital Beltway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Beltway), the city's highway loop constructed in 1964. The phrase is used as a title for a number of political columns and news items by publications, including *[The Washington Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Times)*.[^cite_note-285]

### Budgetary issues



[Muriel Bowser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muriel_Bowser), the city's mayor since 2015

The mayor and council set local taxes and a budget, which Congress must approve. The [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office) and other analysts have estimated that the city's high percentage of tax-exempt property and the Congressional prohibition of commuter taxes create a structural deficit in the district's local budget of anywhere between \$470 million and over \$1 billion per year. Congress typically provides additional grants for federal programs such as [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid) and the operation of the [local justice system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Capital_Revitalization_and_Self-Government_Improvement_Act_of_1997); however, analysts claim that the payments do not fully resolve the imbalance.[^cite_note-DCAppleseed-286][^cite_note-GAO-287]

The city's local government, particularly during the mayoralty of [Marion Barry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion_Barry), has been criticized for mismanagement and waste.[^cite_note-288] During Barry's term as mayor, *[Washington Monthly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monthly)* magazine labeled the city "the worst city government in America" in 1989.[^cite_note-289] In 1995, at the start of Barry's fourth term, Congress created the [District of Columbia Financial Control Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Financial_Control_Board) to oversee all municipal spending.[^cite_note-290] Mayor [Anthony Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_A._Williams) won election in 1998 and oversaw a period of [urban renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_renewal) and budget surpluses.

The district regained control over its finances in 2001 and the oversight board's operations were suspended.[^cite_note-291]

The district has a federally funded "Emergency Planning and Security Fund" to cover security related to visits by foreign leaders and diplomats, presidential inaugurations, protests, and terrorism concerns. During the Trump administration, the fund has run with a deficit. Trump's January 2017 inauguration cost the city \$27 million; of that, \$7 million was never repaid to the fund. Trump's 2019 Independence Day event, "A Salute to America", cost six times more than Independence Day events in past years.[^cite_note-292]

### International relations



The [French ambassador's residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_ambassador's_residence_in_Washington,_D.C.) in the [Sheridan-Kalorama Historic District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheridan-Kalorama_Historic_District)

As the national capital, Washington, D.C. hosts about 185 foreign missions, including embassies, ambassador's residences, and international cultural centers.[^cite_note-293] Many are concentrated along a stretch of [Massachusetts Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Avenue_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) known informally as [Embassy Row](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_Row).[^cite_note-294] Washington, D.C., hosts a number of internationally themed festivals and events, often in collaboration with foreign missions or delegations. The city government maintains an Office of International Affairs to liaise with the diplomatic community and foreign delegations.[^cite_note-295] D.C. has 15 official [sister city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_city) agreements or protocols of friendship.[^cite_note-297]

## Federal voting rights

See also: [District of Columbia voting rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_voting_rights); [Political party strength in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_Washington,_D.C.); and [District of Columbia federal voting rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_federal_voting_rights)



The city's [license plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_registration_plates_of_Washington,_D.C.), which calls for an end to [taxation without representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_without_representation)



The [Washington Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monument) (forefront) and [White House](33057.md) (center) in September 2003. Since 1961, the city's residents can vote for the [U.S. president](24113.md) and [vice president](32759.md), who also serves as [President of the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Senate).



The *[Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statue_of_Abraham_Lincoln_\(Lincoln_Memorial\))* statue at the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial) in September 2016

Washington, D.C., is not a state and therefore has no federal voting representation in [Congress](31756.md). The city's residents elect a [non-voting delegate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-voting_delegate) to the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) ([D.C. at-large](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia's_at-large_congressional_district)), who may sit on committees, participate in debate, and introduce legislation, but cannot vote on the [House floor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floor_\(legislative\)). The district has no official representation in the [United States Senate](24909346.md). Neither chamber seats the district's elected ["shadow" representative or senators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_congressperson). Unlike residents of [U.S. territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._territories) such as [Puerto Rico](23041.md) or [Guam](11974.md), which also have non-voting delegates, D.C. residents are subject to all federal taxes.[^cite_note-298] In the financial year 2012, D.C. residents and businesses paid \$20.7 billion in federal taxes, more than the taxes collected from 19 states and the highest [federal taxes per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_tax_revenue_by_state).[^cite_note-299]

A 2005 poll found that 78% of Americans did not know residents of Washington, D.C., have less representation in Congress than residents of the 50 states.[^cite_note-votepoll-300] Efforts to raise awareness about the issue have included campaigns by [grassroots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassroots) organizations and featuring the city's unofficial motto, "[End Taxation Without Representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_taxation_without_representation)", on [D.C. vehicle license plates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_registration_plates_of_Washington,_D.C.).[^cite_note-301] There is evidence of nationwide approval for D.C. voting rights; various polls indicate that 61 to 82% of Americans believe D.C. should have voting representation in Congress.[^cite_note-votepoll-300][^cite_note-302]

Opponents of federal voting rights for Washington, D.C., propose that the [Founding Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers) never intended for district residents to have a vote in Congress, since the Constitution makes clear that representation must come from the states. Those opposed to making the District of Columbia a state say such a move would destroy the notion of a separate national capital and that statehood would unfairly grant Senate representation to a single city.[^cite_note-303]

The district was granted presidential voting rights by the 23rd Amendment in 1961.[^cite_note-304] The [23rd Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-third_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) was ratified which granted the people of the Washington, D.C., the right to vote for the president. This was done by giving them the same number of Electoral College votes they would get if they were a state, but the number of votes must be no more than the least any state has; this works out to three Electoral College votes. The amendment reads, "A number of electors of President and Vice President equal to the whole number of Senators and Representatives in Congress to which the district would be entitled if it were a State, but in no event more than the least populous State".[^cite_note-305] The 23rd Amendment could complicate statehood, because it would apply even if the federal district was shrunk, and undoing the amendment requires another amendment.[^cite_note-sthd101-62] Congress must operate from a district it controls, but it can be no larger than ten miles on a side; the 2021 statehood bill got around this by proposing the federal district be shrunk to an area roughly the size of the National Mall.[^cite_note-sthd101-62]

In 1978, the [District of Columbia Voting Rights Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Voting_Rights_Amendment) was passed, which would have granted D.C. congressional representation, but it expired in 1986 without being ratified into law.[^cite_note-306] In 2021, a bill was introduced to Congress for [retroceding the district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_retrocession) to Maryland.[^cite_note-307] The idea was that by returning the area to Maryland, the residents would have normal representation as part of a state.[^cite_note-308]

## Education

See also: [District of Columbia Public Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Public_Schools) and [List of parochial and private schools in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parochial_and_private_schools_in_Washington,_D.C.)



[Duke Ellington School of the Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Ellington_School_of_the_Arts), a public [magnet school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnet_school) in the city

[District of Columbia Public Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Public_Schools) (DCPS), the sole public school district in the city,[^cite_note-309] operates the city's 123 public schools.[^cite_note-dcps-310] The number of students in DCPS steadily decreased for 39 years until 2009. In the 2010–11 school year, 46,191 students were enrolled in the public school system.[^cite_note-DCenroll-311] DCPS has one of the highest-cost, yet lowest-performing school systems in the country, in terms of both infrastructure and student achievement.[^cite_note-312] Mayor Adrian Fenty's administration made sweeping changes to the system by closing schools, replacing teachers, firing principals, and using private education firms to aid curriculum development.[^cite_note-313]

The [District of Columbia Public Charter School Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Public_Charter_School_Board) monitors the 52 public charter schools in the city.[^cite_note-314] Due to the perceived problems with the traditional public school system, enrollment in public [charter schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_school) had by 2007 steadily increased.[^cite_note-315] As of 2010, D.C., charter schools had a total enrollment of about 32,000, a 9% increase from the prior year.[^cite_note-DCenroll-311] The district is also home to 92 private schools, which enrolled approximately 18,000 students in 2008.[^cite_note-316]

### Higher education

See also: [List of colleges and universities in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Washington,_D.C.)



[Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University), founded in 1789, the city's oldest university

The [University of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_District_of_Columbia) (UDC) is a public [land-grant university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land-grant_university) providing undergraduate and graduate education.[^cite_note-317] [Federally chartered universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_charter#Federal) include [American University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_University) (AU), [Gallaudet University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallaudet_University), [George Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_University) (GWU), [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University) (GU), and [Howard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_University) (HU). Other private universities include the [Catholic University of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_University_of_America) (CUA), the [Johns Hopkins University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University) [Paul H. Nitze School of Advanced International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_H._Nitze_School_of_Advanced_International_Studies) (SAIS), and [Trinity Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Washington_University). The [Corcoran College of Art and Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcoran_College_of_Art_and_Design), the oldest art school in the capital, was absorbed into the George Washington University in 2014, now serving as its college of arts.[^cite_note-318]

The city's medical research institutions include [Washington Hospital Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Hospital_Center) and [Children's National Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_National_Medical_Center). The city is home to three medical schools and associated teaching hospitals: George Washington, Georgetown, and Howard universities.[^cite_note-319]

### Libraries

Washington, D.C., has dozens of public and private libraries and [library systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_system), including the [District of Columbia Public Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Public_Library) system. [Folger Shakespeare Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folger_Shakespeare_Library), a research library and museum located on [Capitol Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitol_Hill), houses the world's largest collection of material related to [William Shakespeare](32897.md).[^cite_note-320]



The [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress), the [world's largest library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_libraries), has more than 173 million items.

[^cite_note-322]

The [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress) is the [research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_library) [library](17727.md) that officially serves the [United States Congress](31756.md) and is the *[de facto](43594.md)* [national library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_library) of the United States. It is a complex of three buildings: [Thomas Jefferson Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson_Building), [John Adams Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams_Building) and [James Madison Memorial Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison_Memorial_Building), all located in the [Capitol Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitol_Hill) neighborhood. The Jefferson Building houses the library's reading room, a copy of the [Gutenberg Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gutenberg_Bible), [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson)'s original library, and several museum exhibits.

The [District of Columbia Public Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Public_Library) operates 26 neighborhood locations including the landmark [Martin Luther King Jr. Memorial Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Memorial_Library).[^cite_note-323]

## Media

Main article: [Media in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Washington,_D.C.)

See also: [List of newspapers in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Washington,_D.C.); [List of films set in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_set_in_Washington,_D.C.); and [List of television shows set in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_shows_set_in_Washington,_D.C.)



[One Franklin Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Franklin_Square) in [Downtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) houses the headquarters of *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, the [nation's third-largest newspaper by circulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_the_United_States) as of 2023

Washington, D.C., is a prominent center for national and international media. *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, founded in 1877, is the city's most-read local daily newspaper[^cite_note-324] and one of the preeminent newspapers in the United States.[^cite_note-325] It had the sixth-highest readership of all news dailies in the country in 2011.[^cite_note-326] The *Post* previously also published the [Spanish](26825.md)-language newspaper *[El Tiempo Latino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Tiempo_Latino)*, which it sold to [El Planeta Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Planeta_Media) in 2016.[^cite_note-327] The city is served by two local [NPR](29697232.md) member stations, [WAMU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WAMU) and [WETA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WETA_\(FM\)).[^cite_note-328]

*[The Washington Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Times)* is a general interest daily newspaper and popular among [conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-329] The [alternative weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_weekly) *[Washington City Paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_City_Paper)*, with a circulation of 47,000, is also based in the city and has a substantial readership in the Washington area.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331] *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic)* magazine, which has covered politics, international affairs, and cultural issues since 1857, was previously headquartered at the [Watergate complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_complex) but is now headquartered in a building at [the Wharf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wharf_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) in Washington.[^cite_note-332] The headquarters of [Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America), the U.S. government's international news service, is near the Capitol in Southwest Washington, D.C.[^cite_note-333]

Several community and specialty papers focus on neighborhood and cultural issues, including the weekly *[Washington Blade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Blade)* and *[Metro Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Weekly)*, which focus on LGBT issues; the *[Washington Informer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Informer)* and *[The Washington Afro American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Afro_American)*, which highlight topics of interest to the black community; and neighborhood newspapers published by [The Current Newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Current_Newspapers). *[Congressional Quarterly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Quarterly)*, *[The Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hill_\(newspaper\))*, *[Politico](9172097.md)*, and *[Roll Call](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roll_Call)* newspapers focus exclusively on issues related to Congress and the federal government. Other publications based in Washington include the *[National Geographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Geographic)* magazine and political publications such as *[The Washington Examiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Examiner)*, *[The New Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Republic)*, and *[Washington Monthly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Monthly)*.[^cite_note-334]



[CNN](62028.md) reporting from the city during the [2016 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_U.S._presidential_election)

The [Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area) is the ninth-largest television media market in the nation, with two million homes, representing approximately 2% of the country's television market.[^cite_note-335] Several media companies and cable television channels [have their headquarters in the area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area#Media), including *[USA Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Today)*, the [largest newspaper in the country as measured by circulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337]

## Infrastructure

### Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Washington,_D.C.)

#### Streets and highways

Main article: [Streets and highways of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streets_and_highways_of_Washington,_D.C.)

See also: [List of circles in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_circles_in_Washington,_D.C.)



[Pennsylvania Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Avenue), one of the city's most prominent streets, connects the [U.S. Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Capitol) and [White House](33057.md).

There are 1,500 miles (2,400 km) of streets, parkways, and avenues in the district.[^cite_note-338] Due to the [freeway revolts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highway_revolts_in_the_United_States) of the 1960s, much of the proposed [interstate highway system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_highway_system) through the middle of Washington was never built. [Interstate 95](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_95_in_the_District_of_Columbia) (I-95), the nation's major east coast highway, therefore bends around the district to form the eastern portion of the [Capital Beltway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Beltway). A portion of the proposed highway funding was directed to the region's public transportation infrastructure instead.[^cite_note-339] The interstate highways that continue into Washington, including [I-66](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-66) and [I-395](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_395_\(Virginia%E2%80%93District_of_Columbia\)), both terminate shortly after entering the city.[^cite_note-340]

According to a 2010 study, Washington-area commuters spent 70 hours a year in traffic delays, which tied with Chicago for having the nation's worst road congestion.[^cite_note-341] However, 37% of Washington-area commuters take public transportation to work, the second-highest rate in the country.[^cite_note-342] An additional 12% of D.C. commuters walked to work, 6% carpooled, and 3% traveled by bicycle in 2010.[^cite_note-343]

#### Cycling



A [Capital Bikeshare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Bikeshare) rental station near [McPherson Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McPherson_Square)

In May 2022, the city celebrated the expansion of its [bike lane network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycling_infrastructure) to 104 miles (167 km), a 60 percent increase from 2015. Of those miles, 24 miles (39 km) were [protected bike lanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_bike_lane). It also boasted 62 miles (100 km) of [bike trails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bike_trail).[^cite_note-344] As of March 2023, the city has 108 miles (174 km) of bike lanes, with 30 miles (48 km) of them protected bike lanes.[^cite_note-345]

D.C. is part of the regional [Capital Bikeshare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Bikeshare) program. Started in 2010, it is one of the largest [bicycle sharing systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle_sharing_system) in the country. As of February 2024, the program had 6,372 bicycles and 395 stations.[^cite_note-346] A preceding [SmartBike DC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartBike_DC) pilot program had begun in 2008.[^cite_note-347]

#### Walkability

A 2021 study by [Walk Score](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walk_Score) ranked Washington, D.C. the fifth-most walkable city in the country. According to the study, the most walkable neighborhoods are [U Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Street_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), [Dupont Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dupont_Circle), and [Mount Vernon Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Vernon_Square).[^cite_note-348] In 2013, the [Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area) had the eighth lowest percentage of workers who commuted by private automobile (75.7 percent), with 8 percent of area workers traveling via rail transit.[^cite_note-349]

#### River crossings

See also: [List of crossings of the Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_crossings_of_the_Potomac_River) and [List of bridges on the National Register of Historic Places in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bridges_on_the_National_Register_of_Historic_Places_in_Washington,_D.C.)



[Memorial Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington_Memorial_Bridge), connecting the city across the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River) with [Arlington County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington_County)

Bridges that cross the [Potomac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River) and [Anacostia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_River) rivers include [Arlington Memorial Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington_Memorial_Bridge), [14th Street Bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Street_Bridges), [Francis Scott Key Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Scott_Key_Bridge_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), [Theodore Roosevelt Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt_Bridge), [Woodrow Wilson Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Bridge), and [Frederick Douglass Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Douglass_Bridge).[^cite_note-350]

The Wharf Jitney is a free [ferry](50771.md) service operates between [The Wharf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wharf_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) and [East Potomac Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Potomac_Park) during warmer months.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352]

[City Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Cruises) operates [water taxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_taxi) service on the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River) between [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), [Alexandria](18940583.md), The Wharf, and [National Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Harbor).[^cite_note-353]

#### Rail

See also: [List of railroads in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_railroads_in_Washington,_D.C.)



[Washington Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Metro), the [second-busiest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_rapid_transit_systems_by_ridership) rapid rail system in the U.S. based on average weekday ridership, is known for its iconic vaulted ceilings

[Washington Metropolitan Area Transit Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Metropolitan_Area_Transit_Authority) (WMATA) operates [Washington Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Metro), the city's [rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) rail system, which serves Washington, D.C. and its [Maryland](18858.md) and [Northern Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Virginia) suburbs. Metro opened on March 27, 1976, and consists of six lines, [98 stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Washington_Metro_stations), and 129 miles (208 km) of track.[^cite_note-354] Metro is the [second-busiest rapid transit system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_rapid_transit_systems_by_ridership) in the country and [fifth-busiest in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_North_American_rapid_transit_systems_by_ridership).[^cite_note-355] It operates mostly as a deep-level [subway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger_rail_terminology#Subway) in more densely populated areas, while most of the suburban tracks are at surface level or [elevated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elevated). Metro is known for its iconic [brutalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brutalist)-style vaulted ceilings in the interior stations. The longest single-tier escalator in the Western Hemisphere, spanning 230 feet (70 m), is located at Metro's [Wheaton station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheaton_station_\(Washington_Metro\)) in Maryland.[^cite_note-facts_2017-356]

[Washington Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Union_Station), the city's main train station, is Amtrak's 7th busiest, the 11th-busiest in the United States, and 13th-busiest in North America. Approximately 37 million passengers use the station each year.[^cite_note-357] It is the southern terminus for the [Northeast Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Corridor) and multiple [long-distance routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-distance_Amtrak_routes) stop at the station. Maryland's [MARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MARC) and Virginia's [VRE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Railway_Express) commuter trains and the Metrorail [Red Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Line_\(Washington_Metro\)) also provide service into Union Station.[^cite_note-358]

Washington, D.C.'s [streetcars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streetcars_in_Washington,_D.C.), which were a prominent form of transportation in the 19th and early 20th century, were dismantled in the 1960s. In 2016, however, the city brought back a streetcar line, [DC Streetcar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DC_Streetcar), which is a single line system in Northeast Washington, D.C., along [H Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H_Street_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) and [Benning Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benning_Road), known as the [H Street/Benning Road Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H_Street/Benning_Road_Line).[^cite_note-WAPO-359]

#### Bus



[Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrobus_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), operated by the [Washington Metropolitan Area Transit Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Metropolitan_Area_Transit_Authority)

Two main public bus systems operate in Washington, D.C. [Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrobus_\(Washington,_D.C.\)), operated by the [Washington Metropolitan Area Transit Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Metropolitan_Area_Transit_Authority) (WMATA), is the primary public bus system in Washington, D.C. Serving more than 400,000 riders each weekday, it is one of the nation's [largest bus systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_local_bus_agencies_by_ridership) by annual ridership.[^cite_note-360] The city also operated its own [DC Circulator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DC_Circulator) bus system via a public-private partnership between the [District of Columbia Department of Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Department_of_Transportation), WMATA, and DC Surface Transit, Inc. (DCST), which connected commercial and touristic areas within central Washington before its closure on December 31, 2024.[^cite_note-361] The DC Circulator costed \$1 to ride and was composed of six distinct routes that covered central D.C. and suburban [Rosslyn, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosslyn,_Virginia).[^cite_note-362]

There are also numerous commuter buses that residents of the wider Washington region take to commute into the city for work or other events, such as the [Loudoun County Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loudoun_County_Transit) Commuter Bus and the [Maryland Transit Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryland_Transit_Administration) Commuter Bus.[^cite_note-363] The city also has several bus lines used by tourists and others visiting the city, including [Big Bus Tours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bus_Tours), Old Town Trolley Tours, and DC Trails. Many tourists also arrive via charter buses. Following renovations in 2011, Union Station became Washington's primary [intercity bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercity_bus) transit center.[^cite_note-364]

#### Air

See also: [Aviation in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aviation_in_Washington,_D.C.); [List of airports serving Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_serving_Washington,_D.C.); and [List of heliports in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heliports_in_Washington,_D.C.)



[Reagan Washington National Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Washington_National_Airport) in [Arlington, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington,_Virginia) is the closest airport to the city among the three major [Washington metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_metropolitan_area) airports.

Three major airports serve the district, though none are within the city's borders. Two of these major airports are located in suburban [Northern Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Virginia) and one in suburban [Maryland](18858.md). The closest is [Ronald Reagan Washington National Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Washington_National_Airport), which is located in [Arlington County, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington_County,_Virginia), just across the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River) about 5 miles (8 km) from downtown Washington, D.C. This airport provides primarily domestic flights and has the lowest number of passengers of the three airports in the region. The busiest by number of total passengers is [Baltimore/Washington International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore/Washington_International_Airport) (BWI), located in [Anne Arundel County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_Arundel_County), Maryland about 30 miles (48 km) northeast of the city.[^cite_note-365]

The busiest by international flights and the largest by land size and amount of facilities is [Washington Dulles International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Dulles_International_Airport), located in [Dulles, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dulles,_Virginia), about 24 miles (39 km) west of the city.[^cite_note-366] Dulles has the most international passenger traffic of any airport in the [Mid-Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-Atlantic_\(United_States\)) outside the [New York metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area), including approximately 90% of the international passenger traffic in the [Washington-Baltimore region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington%E2%80%93Baltimore_metropolitan_area).[^cite_note-ACI-367] Each of these three airports also serves as a hub for a major American airline: Reagan National Airport is a hub for [American Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines),[^cite_note-368] Dulles is a major hub for [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines) and [Star Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Alliance) partners,[^cite_note-369] and BWI is an operating base for [Southwest Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Airlines).[^cite_note-370] In 2018, the Washington, D.C. area was the [18th-busiest airport system in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_city_airport_systems_by_passenger_traffic) by passenger traffic, accumulating over 74 million passengers between its three main commercial airports; by 2022 it had climbed to 13th-busiest for passenger traffic, even though passenger numbers decreased to less than 69 million.

The [President of the United States](24113.md) does not use any of these airports for travel. Instead, the U.S. president typically travels by [Marine One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_One) from the [South Lawn](15728461.md) of the [White House](33057.md) to [Joint Base Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Base_Andrews) in suburban Maryland. From there, he takes [Air Force One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_One) to his destination.[^cite_note-371]

### Utilities



The [Blue Plains Advanced Wastewater Treatment Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Plains_Advanced_Wastewater_Treatment_Plant) in D.C. is the largest [advanced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sewage_treatment#Tertiary_treatment) wastewater treatment facility in the world.

[^cite_note-372]

The [District of Columbia Water and Sewer Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Water_and_Sewer_Authority), also known as WASA or DC Water, is an independent authority of the Washington, D.C., government that provides drinking water and wastewater collection in the city. WASA purchases water from the historic [Washington Aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Aqueduct), which is operated by the [Army Corps of Engineers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_Corps_of_Engineers). The water, sourced from the [Potomac River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potomac_River), is treated and stored in the city's [Dalecarlia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalecarlia_Reservoir), [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_Reservoir), and [McMillan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McMillan_Reservoir) reservoirs. The aqueduct provides drinking water for a total of 1.1 million people in the district and Virginia, including Arlington, Falls Church, and a portion of Fairfax County.[^cite_note-373]

[Pepco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pepco) is the city's electric utility and services 793,000 customers in the district and suburban Maryland.[^cite_note-374] An 1889 law prohibits overhead wires within much of the historic City of Washington. As a result, all power lines and telecommunication cables are located underground in downtown Washington, and traffic signals are placed at the edge of the street.[^cite_note-375] The [D.C. Public Service Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._Public_Service_Commission) approved a seven-year, \$500 million plan in 2017 to bury more lines underground; construction started in 2019.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377]

[Washington Gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Gas) is the city's [natural gas](22131.md) utility and serves more than a million customers in the district and its suburbs.[^cite_note-378]

## Crime and police

Main article: [Crime in Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Washington,_D.C.)

See also: [List of law enforcement agencies in the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_law_enforcement_agencies_in_the_District_of_Columbia)



[Washington, D.C., police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Police_Department_of_the_District_of_Columbia) on [Harley-Davidson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harley-Davidson) motorcycles introduce the [March for Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_for_Life_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) protest on [Constitution Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Avenue) in January 2018.

Washington has historically endured high crime, particularly violent offences. The city was once described as the "murder capital" of the United States during the early 1990s.[^cite_note-379] The number of murders peaked in 1991 at 479, but then began to decline,[^cite_note-380] reaching a historic low of 88 in 2012, the lowest total since 1961.[^cite_note-381] In 2016, the district's [Metropolitan Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Police_Department_of_the_District_of_Columbia) tallied 135 homicides, a 53% increase from 2012 but a 17% decrease from 2015.[^cite_note-382] Violent crime per-capita has steadily declined since 2012, aside from a spike in 2023 (when D.C. recorded 274 homicides, a 20-year high);[^cite_note-383] in 2024, violent crimes per-capita reached their lowest levels since 2010,[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385] and as of August 2025 police statistics had so far recorded a 7% decrease in overall crime year-over-year, and a 26% decrease in violent crime.[^cite_note-auto1-386][^cite_note-auto-387]

According to a 2018 report, 67,000 residents, or about 10% of the population, are ex-convicts.[^cite_note-388] An estimated 2,000–2,500 offenders return to the city from prison every year.[^cite_note-389]

Many D.C. residents began to press the city government for refusing to prosecute nearly 70% of arrested offenders in 2022. After months of criticism, the rate of unprosecuted cases dropped to 56% by October 2023—albeit still higher than nine of the past 10 years and almost twice what it was in 2013.[^cite_note-390] In February 2024, the [Council of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_District_of_Columbia) passed a major bill meant to reduce crime in the city by introducing harsher penalties for arrested offenders.[^cite_note-391] Rising crime and gang activities contributed to some local businesses leaving the city.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393]

In 2008, the [Supreme Court of the United States](31737.md) held in *[District of Columbia v. Heller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_v._Heller)* that the city's 1976 [handgun ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firearms_Control_Regulations_Act_of_1975) violated the [right to keep and bear arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_keep_and_bear_arms_in_the_United_States) as protected under the [Second Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Amendment).[^cite_note-394] However, [a number of gun control measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_laws_in_the_District_of_Columbia) remain in place, including those requiring firearm registration and banning assault weapons.[^cite_note-395]

In addition to the Metropolitan Police Department, several [federal law enforcement agencies](16252377.md) have jurisdiction in the city, including the [U.S. Park Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Park_Service), founded in 1791.[^cite_note-396] Because the [D.C. National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._National_Guard) serves a federal district, the president of the United States—and not city officials—has power to deploy it. Under Section 740 of the Home Rule Act, the president also has the power to temporarily take over the police force in emergency situations for a maximum of 30 days; this period can be extended with congressional approval.[^cite_note-\:1-397][^cite_note-\:2-398][^cite_note-399] In August 2025, [Donald Trump](4848272.md) invoked Section 740 to declare a public safety emergency.[^cite_note-\:1-397][^cite_note-\:2-398][^cite_note-auto1-386][^cite_note-auto-387]

[^cite_note-10]: &#x20;While both the city and federal district have operated as a union since [1871](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Organic_Act_of_1871), "Washington" historically referred specifically to the city, and "District of Columbia" referred specifically to the federal district.

[^cite_note-11]: &#x20;Washington, D.C. is not part of any [state](18618239.md), it is an independent [federal district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_district_in_the_United_States) (the District of Columbia), which (along with the 50 states) are the constituent parts of the United States of America. The [territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_the_United_States) are not formally [incorporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_the_United_States#Incorporated_vs._unincorporated_territories) in the Union (except the uninhabited [Palmyra Atoll Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_Atoll)).

[^cite_note-24]: &#x20;By 1790, the Southern states had largely repaid their overseas debts from the Revolutionary War. The Northern states had not and wanted the federal government to take over their outstanding liabilities. Southern Congressmen agreed to the plan in return for establishing the new national capital at their preferred site on the Potomac River.<sup>[\[20\]](#cite_note-Crew-22)</sup><sup>: 124 </sup>

[^cite_note-25]: &#x20;The Residence Act allowed the President to select a location within Maryland as far east as the [Anacostia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacostia_River). In 1791, Congress amended the Residence Act to approve the new site, including territory ceded by Virginia.<sup>[\[20\]](#cite_note-Crew-22)</sup><sup>: 89–92 </sup>
